---
title: "Complete Lower-Body Training Encyclopedia"
subtitle: "Anatomy, exercise library, machines, biomechanics, rankings, programs and video tutorials, from hip to toes"
date: "Research compiled 2 November 2026"
---

> **Scope.** Lower-body training only: hips, glutes, thighs, knees, lower legs, ankles, feet and toes, for use as a long-term reference in a well-equipped commercial gym. **Nutrition, diet, protein, supplements, calories and meal planning are deliberately excluded.** Nothing here is medical advice. Sharp, persistent or worsening joint pain needs a physiotherapist or doctor, not a different exercise.

> **How to read this document.** Chapters 3–11 are the exercise libraries, one entry per exercise using a fixed template (A Anatomy · B Equipment · C Starting position · D Execution · E Technique · F Training parameters · G Evaluation · H Alternatives · I Visual learning). Chapter 12 covers machines, Chapter 13 free weights. Chapters 14–21 explain *why* and *what to do*: biomechanics, rankings, progression, warm-up, cooldown, the coverage matrix and the programs. Every exercise has a YouTube search link. A **verified** direct video is given only where its title and channel were confirmed on YouTube during this research (2 November 2026). Where none was confirmed, the entry says so rather than inventing one.

# 1. Executive overview

**The answer in one paragraph.** A complete lower body is built from five movement patterns plus three small "gap" groups that most leg days miss. The patterns are: **(1) a squat or leg press** (quads, glutes, adductors), **(2) a hip hinge** such as the Romanian deadlift (hamstrings, glutes, adductor magnus), **(3) a knee-flexion curl**, ideally *seated* (hamstrings at long length), **(4) a single-leg movement** such as the Bulgarian split squat or step-up (hip and knee control), and **(5) a calf raise**. The gaps are **the hip abductors and rotators** (gluteus medius/minimus, deep six), **the shin and ankle stabilisers** (tibialis anterior/posterior, fibularis) and **the intrinsic foot and toe muscles**. A short accessory block of hip abduction, tibialis raises, banded ankle work, a Copenhagen plank and the short-foot exercise closes almost all of them.

**If you only remember one routine:** leg press or hack squat → Romanian deadlift → seated leg curl → Bulgarian split squat → standing calf raise, plus 10 minutes of hip abduction, tibialis raises and short-foot work twice a week.

**How confident is this?**

| Conclusion | Evidence type | Confidence |
|---|---|---|
| Multi-joint squat/press patterns grow the quadriceps, glutes and adductors | Direct (many RCTs, MRI/ultrasound) | **High** |
| Deeper squats grow the glutes and adductors more than half squats; quads grow similarly | Direct (Kubo et al., 2019) | **Moderate** |
| Seated leg curls grow the hamstrings more than lying leg curls | Direct (Maeo et al., 2021) | **Moderate** |
| The rectus femoris needs knee extension (leg extension) work in addition to squats | Direct: squat training did not significantly grow it (Kubo et al., 2019); knee-extension training does | **Moderate** |
| Hip thrusts and squats produce similar glute growth | Direct (Plotkin et al., 2023, untrained) | **Moderate** |
| Training muscles at long lengths is at least as good as, and often better than, short-length partials | Direct, several small trials (Pedrosa 2022; Kassiano 2023) | **Moderate**, still emerging |
| The Nordic hamstring exercise reduces hamstring injuries | Meta-analysis (van Dyk 2019), later disputed on methods | **Moderate–low** |
| The Copenhagen adduction programme reduces groin problems in footballers | Cluster RCT (Harøy 2019) | **Moderate** (population-specific) |
| Short-foot and toe exercises strengthen foot intrinsics and improve arch control | Small trials (e.g., Mulligan & Cook 2013) | **Low–moderate** |
| Foot position on the leg press or squat can "isolate" the inner/outer quad | **Not supported** | — |

**Bottom line on certainty.** The *patterns* above rest on good evidence. The exact choice between similar machines, bars and dumbbells does not, and effort, progression and consistency matter more than which variant you choose.

# 2. Complete lower-body anatomy

## 2.1 Joint actions you need to know

| Joint | Actions | Main muscles | Typical exercises |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Hip** | Extension (thigh back) | Gluteus maximus, hamstrings (except BF short head), adductor magnus (posterior part) | Hip thrust, RDL, squat |
| | Flexion (thigh forward) | Iliopsoas, rectus femoris, TFL, sartorius, pectineus | Cable hip flexion, hanging knee raise, psoas march |
| | Abduction (thigh out) | Gluteus medius, minimus, TFL, upper glute max | Hip abduction machine, side-lying raise, lateral walk |
| | Adduction (thigh in) | Adductor magnus, longus, brevis, gracilis, pectineus | Adduction machine, Copenhagen plank, sumo squat |
| | External rotation | Piriformis, obturators, gemelli, quadratus femoris ("deep six"), glute max | 90/90 rotations, banded clamshell, cable external rotation |
| | Internal rotation | Glute medius/minimus (anterior fibres), TFL, adductors (position-dependent) | 90/90 internal rotation lifts, seated banded IR |
| **Knee** | Extension (straighten) | Quadriceps (all four heads) | Leg extension, squat, leg press, split squat |
| | Flexion (bend) | Hamstrings, gastrocnemius, gracilis, sartorius, popliteus | Seated/lying leg curl, Nordic curl |
| | Internal rotation (tibia, knee bent) | Popliteus, semitendinosus, semimembranosus, gracilis, sartorius | Seated curl with toes in (minor effect) |
| | External rotation (tibia, knee bent) | Biceps femoris | Seated curl with toes out (minor effect) |
| **Ankle (talocrural)** | Plantarflexion (point foot) | Gastrocnemius, soleus, plantaris; assisted by tibialis posterior, FHL, FDL, fibularis | Calf raises |
| | Dorsiflexion (pull toes up) | Tibialis anterior, EHL, EDL, fibularis tertius | Tibialis raise |
| **Subtalar / midfoot** | Inversion (sole in) | Tibialis posterior, tibialis anterior | Banded inversion |
| | Eversion (sole out) | Fibularis longus, brevis, tertius | Banded eversion |
| **Toes** | Flexion (curl down) | FHL, FDL, FHB, FDB, quadratus plantae, lumbricals (MTP) | Toe curls, towel scrunch |
| | Extension (lift up) | EHL, EDL, EHB, EDB | Toe raises |
| | Spreading / closing | Abductor hallucis, abductor digiti minimi, dorsal interossei (spread); plantar interossei, adductor hallucis (close) | Toe spreading, toe yoga |

## 2.3 A. Gluteal and hip muscles

Illustration: [TeachMeAnatomy — gluteal region](https://teachmeanatomy.info/lower-limb/muscles/gluteal-region/)

| Muscle | Location | Main joint actions | Contribution to movement | How to train it |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Gluteus maximus** | Largest buttock muscle, from ilium/sacrum to IT band and gluteal tuberosity | Hip extension, external rotation; upper fibres abduct, lower fibres adduct | Standing up, climbing, sprinting, locking out a hinge | Hip thrust, deep squat, split squat, RDL, back extension, cable kickback |
| **Gluteus medius** | Upper outer buttock, under the max | Hip abduction; anterior fibres internally rotate, posterior fibres externally rotate | Keeps the pelvis level in single-leg stance (each walking/running step) | Hip abduction machine, side-lying hip abduction, lateral band walk, single-leg work |
| **Gluteus minimus** | Deepest, beneath the medius | Abduction, internal rotation, stabilises femoral head | Hip-joint centring in stance | Same as medius; hip-flexed abduction and internal-rotation work |
| **Tensor fasciae latae (TFL)** | Front outer hip | Hip flexion, abduction, internal rotation; tensions IT band | Assists stance stability | Trained by abduction and flexion; rarely needs direct work |
| **Psoas minor** | Small muscle in front of psoas major; absent in roughly 40–50% of people | Weak trunk/pelvis flexion | Negligible for training | Not trained directly |
| **Iliopsoas** (psoas major + iliacus) | Lumbar spine/inner pelvis to lesser trochanter | Strongest hip flexor; above ~90° of hip flexion it is the main contributor | Lifting the knee in walking, running and stair climbing | Cable or band hip flexion, psoas march, hanging/supine knee raises above 90° |
| **Piriformis** | Sacrum to greater trochanter | External rotation (hip extended); abduction (hip flexed) | Rotational control of femur | 90/90 external rotation, clamshell, cable hip ER |
| **Obturator internus / externus** | Inner/outer surface of obturator membrane to femur | External rotation; femoral head stabilisation | Deep hip stability | As piriformis; loaded ER in varied hip angles |
| **Gemellus superior / inferior** | Small muscles flanking obturator internus | External rotation (with obturator internus) | Deep hip stability | As piriformis |
| **Quadratus femoris** | Ischial tuberosity to intertrochanteric crest | Strong external rotation, some adduction | Deep hip stability | As piriformis |

## 2.4 B. Quadriceps and other anterior-thigh muscles

Illustration: [TeachMeAnatomy — thigh](https://teachmeanatomy.info/lower-limb/muscles/thigh/)

| Muscle | Location | Main joint actions | Contribution | How to train it |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Rectus femoris** | Central front thigh, crosses hip and knee (from AIIS) | Knee extension **and** hip flexion | Kicking, sprint swing phase | Leg extension (critical), reverse Nordic, sissy squat. Squats grow it relatively little because it is shortened at the hip as it lengthens at the knee |
| **Vastus lateralis** | Outer thigh, largest head | Knee extension | Standing, squatting, landing | Squat, leg press, hack squat, split squat, leg extension |
| **Vastus medialis** (incl. "VMO" oblique fibres) | Inner lower thigh, "teardrop" | Knee extension; patellar tracking | Knee stability, especially near lockout | Same as lateralis; it **cannot** be isolated by toes-out positions or terminal-range work |
| **Vastus intermedius** | Deep, beneath rectus femoris | Knee extension | Bulk of quad force | Same as other vasti |
| **Articularis genus** | Tiny slips from the lower front femur to the knee capsule | Lifts the suprapatellar bursa during knee extension | Prevents capsule pinching | Trained automatically by any knee extension |
| **Sartorius** (anterior thigh, not a quadriceps head) | Longest muscle in the body; ASIS diagonally to inner tibia (pes anserinus) | Hip flexion, abduction, external rotation; knee flexion and tibial internal rotation | 'Cross-legged sitting' muscle; medial knee support | Cable hip flexion, leg curls, seated hip abduction; no dedicated exercise needed |

## 2.5 C. Hamstrings

Illustration: [TeachMeAnatomy — thigh](https://teachmeanatomy.info/lower-limb/muscles/thigh/)

| Muscle | Location | Main joint actions | Contribution | How to train it |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Biceps femoris long head** | Outer back thigh, from ischial tuberosity | Hip extension, knee flexion, tibial external rotation | Sprinting (most commonly strained), hip extension | RDL, seated leg curl, Nordic curl, GHR |
| **Biceps femoris short head** | Outer back thigh, from femur only | Knee flexion only (one-joint) | Knee flexion, tibial external rotation | **Only** knee-flexion exercises (leg curls, Nordic); hinges barely train it |
| **Semitendinosus** | Inner back thigh, long thin tendon | Hip extension, knee flexion, tibial internal rotation | Knee flexion, especially at higher speeds | Leg curls (grows strongly with seated and Nordic curls), RDL |
| **Semimembranosus** | Inner back thigh, deep to semitendinosus | Hip extension, knee flexion, tibial internal rotation | Strong hip extensor | RDL, good morning, leg curls |

## 2.6 D. Adductors

Illustration: [TeachMeAnatomy — thigh](https://teachmeanatomy.info/lower-limb/muscles/thigh/)

| Muscle | Location | Main joint actions | Contribution | How to train it |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Adductor magnus** | Largest inner-thigh muscle; posterior ("hamstring") part from ischium | Adduction; posterior part is a powerful hip **extensor** | Rising from a deep squat, hinge lockout | Deep squat, sumo squat, RDL, adduction machine |
| **Adductor longus** | Front inner thigh | Adduction, assists hip flexion | Change of direction, kicking | Adduction machine, Copenhagen plank, cable adduction |
| **Adductor brevis** | Under longus | Adduction | As longus | As longus |
| **Gracilis** | Long strap muscle along inner thigh, crosses knee | Adduction, knee flexion, tibial internal rotation | Medial knee support | Adduction machine, Copenhagen (long lever), leg curls |
| **Pectineus** | Top of inner thigh | Adduction and hip flexion | Hip flexion/adduction combo | Adduction, cable hip flexion |

## 2.7 E. Lower-leg muscles

Illustration: [TeachMeAnatomy — leg](https://teachmeanatomy.info/lower-limb/muscles/leg/)

| Muscle | Location | Main joint actions | Contribution | How to train it |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Gastrocnemius (medial and lateral heads)** | Superficial calf, crosses knee and ankle | Plantarflexion; assists knee flexion | Push-off in walking, running, jumping | **Straight-knee** calf raises (standing, donkey, leg press) |
| **Soleus** | Under gastrocnemius, one-joint | Plantarflexion | Huge endurance role; takes very high loads in running | **Bent-knee** seated calf raise, plus all calf raises |
| **Plantaris** | Small, thin, behind knee (absent in some people) | Weak plantarflexion/knee flexion | Negligible | Trained incidentally by calf raises |
| **Tibialis anterior** | Front shin | Dorsiflexion, inversion | Lifts the foot in swing; controls foot lowering after heel strike | Tibialis raise, banded dorsiflexion |
| **Tibialis posterior** | Deep, back of shin | Inversion, plantarflexion; supports medial arch | Arch support in stance | Banded inversion, heel raise with ball squeeze between ankles |
| **Fibularis (peroneus) longus** | Outer shin, tendon crosses under foot to big-toe base | Eversion, plantarflexion; supports arches; pushes first metatarsal down | Lateral ankle stability, big-toe loading at push-off | Banded eversion, single-leg balance, calf raises with big-toe pressure |
| **Fibularis brevis** | Outer shin to 5th metatarsal | Eversion, plantarflexion | Resists inversion sprains | Banded eversion, balance work |
| **Fibularis tertius** | Front outer shin (variable) | Dorsiflexion, eversion | Toe clearance | Tibialis raise, eversion |
| **Extensor digitorum longus (EDL)** | Front shin to toes 2–5 | Extends toes 2–5, dorsiflexion | Toe clearance | Toe raises, tibialis raise |
| **Flexor digitorum longus (FDL)** | Deep back shin to toes 2–5 | Flexes toes 2–5, inversion, plantarflexion | Toe grip, push-off | Toe curls, calf raises |
| **Extensor hallucis longus (EHL)** | Front shin to big toe | Extends big toe, dorsiflexion | Big-toe lift | Big-toe raises, toe yoga |
| **Flexor hallucis longus (FHL)** | Deep back shin to big toe | Flexes big toe, plantarflexion; supports arch | Final push-off ("toe-off") | Big-toe press, calf raise through the big toe |
| **Popliteus** | Behind knee, deep | Unlocks the extended knee (internal rotation of tibia); resists tibial external rotation | Starting knee flexion from full lockout | Trained by leg curls and controlled knee flexion; rarely needs direct work |

## 2.8 F. Foot and intrinsic muscles

Illustration: [TeachMeAnatomy — foot](https://teachmeanatomy.info/lower-limb/muscles/foot/)

| Muscle | Layer / location | Main action | Function | How to train it |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Abductor hallucis** | 1st plantar layer, inner foot | Abducts and flexes big toe | Main active support of medial arch | Short-foot exercise, toe spreading |
| **Flexor digitorum brevis** | 1st layer, centre of sole | Flexes toes 2–5 (middle joints) | Arch support, toe grip | Toe curls, short foot |
| **Abductor digiti minimi** | 1st layer, outer foot | Abducts/flexes little toe | Lateral column support | Toe spreading |
| **Quadratus plantae** | 2nd layer | Assists FDL, straightening its pull | Toe flexion efficiency | Toe curls |
| **Lumbricals (4)** | 2nd layer | Flex MTP joints, extend IP joints of toes 2–5 | Keeps toes straight while gripping | Toe yoga, "toe press" holds |
| **Interossei (3 plantar, 4 dorsal)** | Between metatarsals | Plantar adduct, dorsal abduct toes 2–5 | Fine toe positioning, forefoot stability | Toe spreading and closing |
| **Flexor hallucis brevis** | 3rd layer | Flexes big toe at MTP | Push-off | Big-toe press-down |
| **Flexor digiti minimi brevis** | 3rd layer, under the 5th metatarsal | Flexes little toe at MTP | Lateral forefoot stability | Toe curls, toe spreading/closing |
| **Adductor hallucis** | 3rd layer, oblique and transverse heads | Adducts big toe; supports transverse arch | Forefoot stability | Toe closing, short foot |
| **Extensor digitorum brevis** | Top of foot | Extends toes 2–4 | Toe lift | Lesser-toe raises |
| **Extensor hallucis brevis** | Top of foot | Extends big toe at MTP | Big-toe lift | Big-toe raises |

## 2.9 Functions of the big toe and lesser toes

| Toe(s) | Joints | Muscles | Function in movement | Practical training |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Big toe (hallux)** | MTP + one IP joint | FHL, FHB, EHL, EHB, abductor and adductor hallucis | Takes a large share of forefoot load at push-off; its extension (about 50–60° needed in walking) winds up the plantar fascia ("windlass mechanism") and stiffens the arch | Big-toe press with lesser toes lifted; calf raises finishing over the big toe; big-toe mobility |
| **Second–fifth toes** | MTP + two IP joints each | FDL, FDB, EDL, EDB, lumbricals, interossei, abductor digiti minimi | Widen the base of support, help balance, share push-off load; lumbricals keep toes straight instead of clawing | Lesser-toe press with big toe lifted (toe yoga); toe spreading; towel curls |

Evidence note: toe flexor strength has been associated with balance and fall risk in older adults in observational research, but that does not prove that training it improves performance in young lifters. The training here is low-cost insurance rather than a proven performance tool.

## 2.10 Research method and evidence hierarchy

1. **Direct long-term evidence**: training studies measuring muscle size (MRI/ultrasound) or strength in the specific muscle.
2. **Systematic reviews and meta-analyses** pooling those studies.
3. **Acute evidence**: EMG, muscle swelling, moment-arm modelling. Useful for *hypotheses*, never sufficient by itself for ranking growth.
4. **Anatomy and biomechanics**: line of pull, moment arms, muscle length.
5. **Expert coaching opinion**: labelled as such.

Where only levels 3–5 support a claim, it is marked *(coaching consensus)* or *(biomechanical inference)*.

# 3. Glute and hip exercise library

The gluteal group needs three things: a **long-length** hip-extension stimulus (deep squat, split squat, RDL — see Chapters 4–5), a **short-length** peak-contraction stimulus (hip thrust, bridge, kickback) and **abduction** work for the glute medius and minimus. The deep rotators and iliopsoas are rarely trained; entries 3.9 and 3.10 cover them.

| # | Exercise | Category | Level |
|---|---|---|---|
| 3.1 | Barbell Hip Thrust | Compound · Barbell · Bilateral | Beginner → Advanced |
| 3.2 | Glute Bridge (bodyweight or dumbbell) | Bodyweight / Dumbbell · Bilateral | Beginner |
| 3.3 | Cable Glute Kickback | Isolation · Cable · Unilateral | Beginner → Intermediate |
| 3.4 | Seated Hip Abduction (machine) | Isolation · Machine | Beginner |
| 3.5 | Side-Lying Hip Abduction | Bodyweight · Isolation · Rehab-oriented | Beginner |
| 3.6 | Banded Lateral Walk (Monster Walk) | Resistance band · Functional · Activation | Beginner |
| 3.7 | 45° Back Extension (glute-biased) | Compound · Machine/bench · Bodyweight-loadable | Beginner → Intermediate |
| 3.8 | Reverse Hyperextension | Machine · Posterior chain | Intermediate |
| 3.9 | Cable / Band Hip Flexion and Psoas March | Isolation · Cable/Band · Unilateral | Beginner |
| 3.10 | Hip Rotation Series (90/90 lifts, banded clamshell, cable rotations) | Mobility · Stability · Rehab-oriented | Beginner |
| 3.11 | Kettlebell Swing | Functional · Kettlebell · Ballistic | Intermediate |

## 3.1 Barbell Hip Thrust

**Category:** Compound · Barbell · Bilateral · **Level:** Beginner → Advanced

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** Gluteus maximus
- **Secondary:** Hamstrings, adductor magnus, quadriceps (modest), gluteus medius
- **Specific muscles:** Gluteus maximus (upper and lower fibres), semitendinosus, semimembranosus, biceps femoris long head, adductor magnus posterior head
- **Joint actions:** Hip extension (main), slight posterior pelvic tilt at lockout; knee held at ~90°

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| No | — | Optional (light substitute) | Yes | No | Optional (around knees) | Glute bridge / single-leg hip thrust |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** Upper back (just below shoulder blades) on a bench ~40 cm high, chin slightly tucked, ribs down.
- **Foot position:** Flat, about hip-width, toes slightly out; shins vertical at the top of the rep.
- **Hands / grip:** Hands just outside the hips holding the bar; use a thick bar pad.
- **Machine / setup adjustments:** Roll the bar over the hips while seated; a hip-thrust bench with a belt makes setup easier.
- **Starting range:** Hips just off the floor, torso and shins forming a 'V'.

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Seated, bar over hip crease, upper back on the bench edge.
2. **Initiate.** Drive through the whole foot (heels and mid-foot) and squeeze the glutes before the bar moves.
3. **Movement path.** Hips rise vertically; the upper back pivots on the bench while the chin stays tucked so the ribs and pelvis move together.
4. **Breathing.** Breathe in and brace before each rep; exhale near lockout.
5. **Contraction.** Peak glute contraction as the hips reach full extension.
6. **End position.** Torso parallel to floor, knees ~90°, shins vertical, slight posterior pelvic tilt — no arching of the low back.
7. **Return.** Lower under control over 2 s until the hips are close to the floor.
8. **Recommended ROM.** Full hip extension at top to ~60–90° hip flexion at bottom.

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** Knees track over the 2nd–3rd toes; chin and ribs tucked; don't hyperextend the lumbar spine to 'finish'.
- **Tempo:** 1 s up, 1 s pause at top, 2 s down.
- **Muscle engagement:** If you feel mostly hamstrings, move the feet closer; if mostly quads, move them further away.

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Arching the lower back at the top | Tuck chin, keep ribs down, finish with the glutes (slight posterior tilt) |
| Bench too high or too low | Bench edge should sit at the bottom of the shoulder blades |
| Pushing through toes | Keep heels down; lift toes slightly if needed |
| Bar slides or hurts | Use a thick pad or a hip-thrust belt machine |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2–3 × 10–15, light | 3–4 × 8–12 | 3–5 × 6–12, plus pauses or single-leg work | 2–3 min | Add reps to the top of the range, then add 5–10 kg |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| High | High (hip extension) | Good once set up | Low | Moderate (needs bench + pad) | Very safe for knees and spine when not hyperextended |

- **Advantages:** Heavy glute loading with little spinal compression or balance demand; easy to overload
- **Disadvantages:** Awkward setup; glutes are loaded most at short length, so pair with a deep squat or split squat

**H. Alternatives**

Machine: Hip-thrust machine / Glute Drive · Dumbbell: Dumbbell hip thrust · Bodyweight: Single-leg hip thrust · Easier: Glute bridge · Harder: Paused or banded hip thrust, B-stance thrust

**I. Visual learning**

| Item | Resource |
|---|---|
| Best tutorial (verified) | [How To Hip Thrust (Proper Form And Mistakes To Avoid)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=z8WwUC_UA4w) — *Markow Training Systems* |
| Video type / learning focus | Full tutorial. Bench height, foot position, lockout and the most common faults |
| Most suitable educators | Bret Contreras (inventor of the hip thrust): [channel](https://www.youtube.com/c/bretcontreras1); Jeff Nippard for evidence context |
| YouTube search | [barbell hip thrust tutorial form](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=barbell+hip+thrust+tutorial+form) |
| Shorts (search) | [barbell hip thrust tutorial form shorts](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=barbell+hip+thrust+tutorial+form+%23shorts) — no individual Short verified |
| Demonstration image search | [Images: Barbell Hip Thrust](https://duckduckgo.com/?ia=images&iax=images&q=Barbell+Hip+Thrust+exercise) |
| Anatomy illustration | [TeachMeAnatomy — gluteal region](https://teachmeanatomy.info/lower-limb/muscles/gluteal-region/) · [Kenhub lower-limb atlas](https://www.kenhub.com/en/library/anatomy/lower-extremity-anatomy) |

## 3.2 Glute Bridge (bodyweight or dumbbell)

**Category:** Bodyweight / Dumbbell · Bilateral · **Level:** Beginner

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** Gluteus maximus
- **Secondary:** Hamstrings, adductor magnus
- **Specific muscles:** Gluteus maximus, semitendinosus, biceps femoris long head
- **Joint actions:** Hip extension from the floor

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| No | — | Optional | Optional | No | Optional (above knees) | Is bodyweight |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** Lying on back, arms at sides, ribs down.
- **Foot position:** Flat, hip-width, heels ~20–30 cm from hips.
- **Hands / grip:** Palms on the floor, or holding a dumbbell on the hips.
- **Starting range:** Hips on the floor.

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Lie with knees bent and feet flat.
2. **Initiate.** Press heels into the floor and squeeze the glutes.
3. **Movement path.** Lift the hips in a straight line until knees, hips and shoulders align.
4. **Breathing.** Exhale on the way up.
5. **Contraction.** Hold 1–2 s with the glutes tight.
6. **End position.** Straight line from knees to shoulders; no low-back arch.
7. **Return.** Lower slowly to touch the floor.
8. **Recommended ROM.** Floor to full hip extension.

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** Knees stay hip-width (a band helps).
- **Tempo:** 1 s up, 2 s hold, 2 s down.
- **Muscle engagement:** Think 'push the floor away with the heels'.

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Over-arching | Stop at a straight line; tuck the pelvis slightly |
| Hamstring cramping | Bring feet slightly closer and lift toes less |
| Knees collapsing in | Add a mini-band and push knees out |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2–3 × 12–20 | 3 × 15–20 with dumbbell or single leg | Single-leg 3 × 10–15 with load | 60–90 s | Bodyweight → single leg → dumbbell → barbell hip thrust |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Moderate | Low–moderate | Excellent | Low | Excellent (no equipment) | Very safe |

- **Advantages:** Teaches glute-led hip extension; can be done anywhere; ideal warm-up
- **Disadvantages:** Short range from the floor; quickly too easy without load

**H. Alternatives**

Machine: Hip-thrust machine · Dumbbell: Dumbbell hip thrust · Bodyweight: Single-leg glute bridge · Easier: Bridge hold (isometric) · Harder: Barbell hip thrust

**I. Visual learning**

| Item | Resource |
|---|---|
| Best tutorial (verified) | [How To Hip Thrust (Proper Form And Mistakes To Avoid)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=z8WwUC_UA4w) — *Markow Training Systems* |
| Video type / learning focus | Related tutorial (hip thrust). Same glute-led hip extension cues, progressed from the bridge |
| Most suitable educators | Bret Contreras; physiotherapist channels for rehab variations |
| YouTube search | [glute bridge proper form](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=glute+bridge+proper+form) |

## 3.3 Cable Glute Kickback

**Category:** Isolation · Cable · Unilateral · **Level:** Beginner → Intermediate

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** Gluteus maximus
- **Secondary:** Hamstrings, gluteus medius (stabilising leg)
- **Specific muscles:** Gluteus maximus (mainly upper/middle fibres when kicking slightly out to the side)
- **Joint actions:** Hip extension (with slight abduction if the leg travels back-and-out)

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Yes | Cable pulley (low position) or dedicated glute-kickback machine | No | No | Yes | Alternative | Quadruped donkey kick |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** Hinge forward ~30–45°, hands on the cable frame, spine neutral.
- **Foot position:** Ankle cuff on the working leg; stand on the other leg with a soft knee.
- **Hands / grip:** Hold the frame for balance.
- **Machine / setup adjustments:** Pulley at the lowest setting.
- **Starting range:** Working hip flexed ~30–45° (foot slightly in front).

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Hinge, brace, working foot slightly forward.
2. **Initiate.** Start by squeezing the glute, not by arching the back.
3. **Movement path.** Kick the leg back and slightly out in an arc, knee slightly bent.
4. **Breathing.** Exhale as the leg goes back.
5. **Contraction.** Squeeze at full hip extension.
6. **End position.** Thigh just behind the body line — stop before the lower back arches.
7. **Return.** Return under control until the hip is flexed again.
8. **Recommended ROM.** From ~45° hip flexion to just past neutral.

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** Pelvis stays square; no rotation.
- **Tempo:** 1 s back, 1 s squeeze, 2 s return.
- **Muscle engagement:** A forward torso lean allows more hip flexion at the start (longer glute length).

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Swinging from the lower back | Reduce load; stop at neutral hip extension |
| Rotating the pelvis open | Keep hips square to the machine |
| Standing upright | Lean forward so the glute is stretched at the start |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 × 12–15 / leg | 3 × 10–15 / leg | 3–4 × 10–20 / leg, lengthened partials | 60–90 s | Add reps, then a plate step; add a pause at top |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Moderate | Low | Good | Moderate (balance) | Good (any cable) | Safe if spine stays neutral |

- **Advantages:** Direct, low-fatigue glute volume; easy to do unilaterally
- **Disadvantages:** Small load capacity; easy to cheat with the back

**H. Alternatives**

Machine: Glute-kickback machine · Dumbbell: — · Bodyweight: Quadruped donkey kick · Easier: Banded kickback · Harder: Deficit/forward-lean kickback with pause

**I. Visual learning**

| Item | Resource |
|---|---|
| Best tutorial | No direct video verified in this research; use the search link (prefer E3 Rehab, Squat University, Jeff Nippard or a physiotherapist channel in the results) |
| Most suitable educators | Jeff Nippard, Jeremy Ethier |
| YouTube search | [cable glute kickback form](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=cable+glute+kickback+form) |

## 3.4 Seated Hip Abduction (machine)

**Category:** Isolation · Machine · **Level:** Beginner

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** Gluteus medius, gluteus minimus
- **Secondary:** Upper gluteus maximus, TFL
- **Specific muscles:** Gluteus medius (all fibres), gluteus minimus, superior fibres of gluteus maximus, TFL, piriformis (hip flexed)
- **Joint actions:** Hip abduction (with the hip flexed ~90° when seated)

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Yes | Hip abduction machine (outer thigh) | No | No | Alternative | Alternative | Side-lying hip abduction |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** Seated upright, back on the pad; a slight forward lean shifts emphasis to upper glute max.
- **Foot position:** On the foot rests.
- **Hands / grip:** On the handles.
- **Machine / setup adjustments:** Pads on the outside of the knees; set the start so legs are close together with tension.
- **Starting range:** Thighs together.

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Sit tall, pads outside knees.
2. **Initiate.** Push the knees out by squeezing the side of the hips.
3. **Movement path.** Move the thighs outward in an arc.
4. **Breathing.** Exhale out, inhale back.
5. **Contraction.** Squeeze at the widest point.
6. **End position.** As wide as you can control.
7. **Return.** Return slowly; don't let the weight stack slam.
8. **Recommended ROM.** Full available range; the inner (thighs-together) position matters.

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** Hips stay in the seat; no bouncing.
- **Tempo:** 1 s out, 1 s hold, 2–3 s in.
- **Muscle engagement:** Leaning forward (~30°) tends to increase glute max involvement (coaching consensus).

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Using momentum | Slow the eccentric to 2–3 s |
| Partial range at the wide end only | Use the full range |
| Lifting hips off the seat | Reduce load |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2–3 × 15–20 | 3 × 12–20 | 3–4 × 12–25, drop sets | 60 s | Add reps then plates |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Moderate (glute med/min) | Low | Excellent | Very low | Good (most gyms) | Very safe |

- **Advantages:** The simplest way to load the glute medius and minimus directly
- **Disadvantages:** Seated, hip-flexed position differs from standing single-leg function

**H. Alternatives**

Machine: Cable standing hip abduction · Dumbbell: Side-lying abduction with ankle weight · Bodyweight: Side-lying hip abduction · Easier: Banded seated abduction · Harder: Standing cable abduction, lean-away

**I. Visual learning**

## 3.5 Side-Lying Hip Abduction

**Category:** Bodyweight · Isolation · Rehab-oriented · **Level:** Beginner

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** Gluteus medius
- **Secondary:** Gluteus minimus, TFL
- **Specific muscles:** Posterior and middle gluteus medius (when the leg is slightly behind the body)
- **Joint actions:** Hip abduction with the hip extended

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| No | — | No | No | No | Optional | Is bodyweight |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** Lying on side, body in a straight line, bottom knee bent for stability.
- **Foot position:** Top leg straight, toes pointing forward or slightly down.
- **Hands / grip:** Top hand on the hip to monitor rolling.
- **Starting range:** Top leg resting on bottom leg.

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Lie on your side, pelvis stacked.
2. **Initiate.** Lift the top leg by squeezing the side of the hip.
3. **Movement path.** Raise the leg slightly behind the body line, heel leading.
4. **Breathing.** Exhale on the lift.
5. **Contraction.** Hold 1–2 s at about 30–40°.
6. **End position.** Leg in line with or slightly behind the torso.
7. **Return.** Lower slowly without resting.
8. **Recommended ROM.** ~0–40° abduction (more means the trunk is side-bending).

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** Don't roll back; don't let the leg drift forward (that turns it into TFL/hip-flexor work).
- **Tempo:** 1 s up, 2 s hold, 2 s down.
- **Muscle engagement:** EMG studies rank this among the highest-activation glute medius exercises (Distefano et al., 2009).

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Leg drifts forward | Keep heel slightly behind the body |
| Rolling the pelvis back | Hand on hip; stack hips vertically |
| Lifting too high | Stop when the pelvis starts to move |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 × 12–15 / side | 3 × 15–20 with band or ankle weight | 3 × 15–25 heavier | 45–60 s | Bodyweight → band → ankle weight → cable |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Low–moderate | Low | Excellent | Low | Excellent | Very safe |

- **Advantages:** High glute medius activation for zero equipment; good for knee and hip rehab
- **Disadvantages:** Small loads; low hypertrophy potential in trained people

**H. Alternatives**

Machine: Hip abduction machine · Dumbbell: Ankle-weight version · Bodyweight: This is the bodyweight option · Easier: Clamshell · Harder: Side-plank with top-leg abduction

**I. Visual learning**

## 3.6 Banded Lateral Walk (Monster Walk)

**Category:** Resistance band · Functional · Activation · **Level:** Beginner

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** Gluteus medius
- **Secondary:** Gluteus minimus, TFL, upper glute max, quadriceps (isometric)
- **Specific muscles:** Gluteus medius of both the moving and the stance leg
- **Joint actions:** Hip abduction and isometric hip/knee stability in a quarter squat

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| No | — | No | No | No | Yes (mini-band above knees or around ankles/feet) | Lateral shuffle without band |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** Quarter squat, chest up, hips back slightly.
- **Foot position:** Hip-width, toes forward; band tension already present.
- **Hands / grip:** On hips or in front.
- **Starting range:** Feet hip-width.

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Place a mini-band above the knees (easier) or around the forefeet (harder), feet hip-width.
2. **Initiate.** Sit into a quarter squat and push the lead knee out against the band.
3. **Movement path.** Step sideways 20–30 cm with the lead foot, then follow with the trailing foot only far enough to keep tension.
4. **Breathing.** Breathe steadily; keep the trunk braced.
5. **Contraction.** Feel the side of both hips — the stance-side glute medius works as hard as the stepping side.
6. **End position.** Feet never come fully together; torso stays level without rocking.
7. **Return.** Take the same number of steps back in the other direction.
8. **Recommended ROM.** 10–15 steps each way per set.

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** Knees stay over toes; torso level, no rocking side to side.
- **Tempo:** Slow, controlled steps.
- **Muscle engagement:** Band around the feet raises glute activation compared with above the knees (biomechanical/EMG evidence).

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Rocking the torso | Smaller steps, stay low |
| Knees caving | Push knees out into the band |
| Feet snapping together | Keep band taut throughout |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 × 10 steps each way | 3 × 15 each way, heavier band | Use as warm-up only | 30–60 s | Heavier band, band at feet, deeper squat |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Low | Low | Excellent | Moderate | Excellent (band only) | Very safe |

- **Advantages:** Great activation and warm-up drill; trains frontal-plane control
- **Disadvantages:** Not an efficient hypertrophy exercise

**H. Alternatives**

Machine: Hip abduction machine · Dumbbell: — · Bodyweight: Lateral shuffle · Easier: Band above knees · Harder: Band around forefoot, deeper squat

## 3.7 45° Back Extension (glute-biased)

**Category:** Compound · Machine/bench · Bodyweight-loadable · **Level:** Beginner → Intermediate

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** Gluteus maximus, hamstrings
- **Secondary:** Erector spinae, adductor magnus
- **Specific muscles:** Glute max, semitendinosus/semimembranosus, biceps femoris long head; erectors isometrically
- **Joint actions:** Hip extension (spine held neutral or slightly rounded for glute bias)

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Yes | 45° hyperextension bench | Optional (hold to chest) | No | No | Optional | Is bodyweight-loadable |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** Hips on the pad, pad top just below the hip crease so the hips can bend.
- **Foot position:** Under the ankle rollers; turning toes out ~30° and pushing knees out biases glutes (coaching consensus).
- **Hands / grip:** Crossed on chest or holding a plate.
- **Machine / setup adjustments:** Pad height so the pelvis can rotate over it freely.
- **Starting range:** Body in a straight line.

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Body straight, glutes squeezed.
2. **Initiate.** Hinge at the hips (not the lower back).
3. **Movement path.** Lower the torso until hamstrings are stretched (~60–90° hip flexion).
4. **Breathing.** Inhale down, exhale up.
5. **Contraction.** Drive the hips into the pad with the glutes to rise.
6. **End position.** Straight line from head to heels — not hyperextended.
7. **Return.** Lower slowly.
8. **Recommended ROM.** Full hip range; spine neutral.

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** For glute bias: slight upper-back rounding, toes out; for erector bias: neutral spine, toes forward.
- **Tempo:** 2 s down, 1 s up, 1 s squeeze.
- **Muscle engagement:** Push the pad with the front of the hips.

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Hyperextending at the top | Stop at a straight line |
| Pad too high | Lower it so you can hinge |
| Moving from the lumbar spine | Think 'hips back, hips forward' |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2–3 × 12–15 bodyweight | 3 × 10–15 with plate | 3–4 × 8–15 heavy/DB | 90 s | Bodyweight → plate → dumbbell → band + plate |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Moderate–high | Moderate | Good | Low | Good | Safe with neutral spine |

- **Advantages:** Loads glutes and hamstrings at long length with low spinal compression
- **Disadvantages:** Some lifters feel it mostly in the lower back

**H. Alternatives**

Machine: Reverse hyperextension · Dumbbell: Dumbbell RDL · Bodyweight: Prone hip extension · Easier: Bodyweight, short range · Harder: Single-leg 45° back extension

|---|---|

## 3.8 Reverse Hyperextension

**Category:** Machine · Posterior chain · **Level:** Intermediate

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** Gluteus maximus, hamstrings
- **Secondary:** Erector spinae
- **Specific muscles:** Glute max, hamstring long heads, lumbar erectors (dynamic, with spinal traction at the bottom)
- **Joint actions:** Hip extension with the torso fixed

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Optional | Reverse hyperextension machine (pendulum) — or a high bench | Optional (between feet, on bench version) | No | No | Optional | Bench reverse hyper |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** Torso prone on the pad, hips at the edge, holding handles.
- **Foot position:** Ankles in the strap/roller.
- **Hands / grip:** Grip handles firmly.
- **Machine / setup adjustments:** Start with light weight; strap at ankles.
- **Starting range:** Legs hanging, hips flexed.

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Lie prone, hips off the edge.
2. **Initiate.** Squeeze glutes to lift the legs.
3. **Movement path.** Swing the legs up to body level in a controlled arc.
4. **Breathing.** Exhale up.
5. **Contraction.** Hold briefly at the top.
6. **End position.** Legs in line with the torso; no hyperextension.
7. **Return.** Lower under control — do not let the pendulum throw you.
8. **Recommended ROM.** Hip flexion to neutral.

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** Upper body still.
- **Tempo:** 1 s up, 1 s hold, 2 s down.
- **Muscle engagement:** Glutes start the movement; the back only stabilises.

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Swinging the pendulum | Control the down phase; lighter load |
| Arching hard at the top | Stop at body level |
| Too heavy too soon | Learn the pattern with bodyweight |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 × 12–15 light | 3 × 10–15 | 3–4 × 8–12 | 90 s | Small load steps |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Moderate | Moderate | Fair | Low | Low (specialised) | Safe if controlled |

- **Advantages:** Hip extension without spinal compression
- **Disadvantages:** Specialised machine; momentum is tempting

**H. Alternatives**

Machine: 45° back extension · Dumbbell: Bench reverse hyper with dumbbell between feet · Bodyweight: Prone leg lift on bench · Easier: Bodyweight bench version · Harder: Single-leg reverse hyper

## 3.9 Cable / Band Hip Flexion and Psoas March

**Category:** Isolation · Cable/Band · Unilateral · **Level:** Beginner

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** Iliopsoas
- **Secondary:** Rectus femoris, TFL, pectineus, sartorius
- **Specific muscles:** Psoas major and iliacus, especially above 90° of hip flexion
- **Joint actions:** Hip flexion

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Yes | Low cable pulley with ankle cuff | No | No | Yes | Yes (psoas march) | Standing knee raise hold |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** Standing tall facing away from the cable (or lying supine for the band psoas march).
- **Foot position:** Cuff on working ankle; stance leg straight.
- **Hands / grip:** Hold the frame.
- **Machine / setup adjustments:** Pulley low.
- **Starting range:** Working leg slightly behind body.

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Stand tall, ribs down.
2. **Initiate.** Lift the knee by pulling the thigh up from the hip.
3. **Movement path.** Bring the knee up above hip height.
4. **Breathing.** Exhale on lift.
5. **Contraction.** Hold 1–2 s at the top (the iliopsoas works hardest here).
6. **End position.** Thigh above horizontal, torso upright.
7. **Return.** Lower slowly.
8. **Recommended ROM.** From slight extension to >90° hip flexion.

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** No leaning back or rounding.
- **Tempo:** 1 s up, 2 s hold, 2 s down.
- **Muscle engagement:** High-knee holds without hands (psoas holds) bias the iliopsoas because the rectus femoris is weak at high hip flexion.

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Leaning back | Stand tall with core braced |
| Swinging the leg | Hold at top |
| Rounding the low back | Keep ribs and pelvis stacked |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 × 10 / leg | 3 × 10–15 / leg | 3 × 8–12 with holds | 60 s | More load, longer holds |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Low–moderate | Moderate (hip flexors) | Good | Moderate | Good | Safe |

- **Advantages:** Directly trains a muscle most leg days ignore; important for running and stair climbing
- **Disadvantages:** Small hypertrophy value; can irritate a sensitive hip front

**H. Alternatives**

Machine: Hip-flexion machine / captain's chair · Dumbbell: — · Bodyweight: Hanging or supine knee raise · Easier: Seated knee lift · Harder: Hanging straight-leg raise

## 3.10 Hip Rotation Series (90/90 lifts, banded clamshell, cable rotations)

**Category:** Mobility · Stability · Rehab-oriented · **Level:** Beginner

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** Deep six external rotators (piriformis, obturator internus/externus, gemelli, quadratus femoris)
- **Secondary:** Gluteus medius/minimus (internal rotation), gluteus maximus
- **Specific muscles:** Piriformis, obturator internus, obturator externus, gemellus superior/inferior, quadratus femoris; anterior glute med/min and TFL for internal rotation
- **Joint actions:** Hip external and internal rotation at different hip angles

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| No | — | No | No | Optional (seated cable rotations) | Yes (clamshell) | 90/90 hip lifts |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** 90/90: sitting with front leg and back leg both bent 90°, torso upright. Clamshell: side-lying, hips and knees bent ~45°.
- **Foot position:** 90/90: feet stay on the floor. Clamshell: heels together.
- **Hands / grip:** 90/90: hands behind or none.
- **Starting range:** Neutral rotation.

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Set the position.
2. **Initiate.** 90/90: lift the back knee off the floor (internal rotation) or the front knee (external rotation). Clamshell: lift the top knee.
3. **Movement path.** Rotate in a slow arc without rolling the pelvis.
4. **Breathing.** Steady breathing.
5. **Contraction.** Hold at end range 2–3 s.
6. **End position.** As far as you can rotate without trunk compensation.
7. **Return.** Lower slowly.
8. **Recommended ROM.** Full active rotation range.

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** Pelvis still; the motion comes from the femur rotating in the socket.
- **Tempo:** Slow, 2–3 s holds.
- **Muscle engagement:** These are low-load control exercises; the goal is active range, not fatigue.

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Rolling the pelvis back (clamshell) | Stack hips; hand on top hip |
| Leaning to compensate (90/90) | Use hands for support until strength improves |
| Rushing | Hold end range |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 × 8 / side | 3 × 10 / side with band | 3 × 10 with pauses and load | 30–45 s | Longer holds, band, hands-free 90/90 |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Low | Low | Excellent | Low | Excellent | Very safe |

- **Advantages:** Only practical way to directly train deep rotators and rotational control
- **Disadvantages:** Minimal hypertrophy; slow to show results

**H. Alternatives**

Machine: Seated cable hip rotation · Dumbbell: — · Bodyweight: 90/90 transitions · Easier: Clamshell without band · Harder: Standing banded hip rotation, Copenhagen side plank

## 3.11 Kettlebell Swing

**Category:** Functional · Kettlebell · Ballistic · **Level:** Intermediate

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** Gluteus maximus, hamstrings
- **Secondary:** Adductor magnus, erector spinae, core, grip
- **Specific muscles:** Glute max, biceps femoris long head, semitendinosus, semimembranosus
- **Joint actions:** Explosive hip extension (hip hinge), knees slightly bent

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| No | — | Alternative (dumbbell swing) | No | No | No | Broad jump |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** Hinge with flat back, bell ~30 cm in front.
- **Foot position:** Slightly wider than hips.
- **Hands / grip:** Both hands on the handle.
- **Starting range:** Bell on the floor in front.

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Hike the bell back between the legs like a centre snap.
2. **Initiate.** Snap the hips forward explosively.
3. **Movement path.** Bell floats to chest height from hip power, arms relaxed.
4. **Breathing.** Sharp exhale at the top.
5. **Contraction.** Glutes and quads lock out hard.
6. **End position.** Standing tall, body vertical, bell at chest height.
7. **Return.** Let the bell fall; hinge as forearms meet the hips.
8. **Recommended ROM.** Hinge range, not a squat.

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** Neutral spine; shins nearly vertical.
- **Tempo:** Explosive.
- **Muscle engagement:** Power from the hips, not the arms.

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Squatting the swing | Hips back, shins vertical |
| Lifting with arms | Arms are ropes |
| Leaning back at the top | Stand tall, ribs down |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Learn hinge first; 3 × 10 light | 4–5 × 12–15 | Heavy 5 × 10 or EMOM | 60–90 s | Heavier bell, then single-arm |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Moderate | Moderate (power) | Fair (technical) | Moderate | Good | Safe with good hinge |

- **Advantages:** Develops hip power and conditioning
- **Disadvantages:** Not a top hypertrophy choice; needs coaching

**H. Alternatives**

Machine: — · Dumbbell: Dumbbell swing · Bodyweight: Broad jump · Easier: Hip hinge drill / dowel hinge · Harder: Single-arm swing

# 4. Quadriceps exercise library

All four quadriceps heads extend the knee, but only the rectus femoris also flexes the hip. Squat patterns grow the three vasti well and the rectus femoris relatively little, so a complete quad plan combines **one squat or press pattern** with **a knee-extension exercise**.

| # | Exercise | Category | Level |
|---|---|---|---|
| 4.1 | Barbell Back Squat | Compound · Barbell · Bilateral | Intermediate → Advanced |
| 4.2 | Barbell Front Squat | Compound · Barbell | Intermediate → Advanced |
| 4.3 | Goblet Squat | Compound · Dumbbell/Kettlebell · Bilateral | Beginner |
| 4.4 | Hack Squat (machine) | Compound · Machine | Beginner → Advanced |
| 4.5 | 45-Degree Leg Press | Compound · Machine | Beginner → Advanced |
| 4.6 | Leg Extension | Isolation · Machine | Beginner → Advanced |
| 4.7 | Bulgarian Split Squat | Compound · Dumbbell/Barbell · Unilateral | Intermediate |
| 4.8 | Smith Machine Squat | Compound · Machine | Beginner → Intermediate |
| 4.9 | Sissy Squat / Reverse Nordic | Bodyweight · Isolation (quads) | Intermediate → Advanced |
| 4.10 | Spanish Squat | Resistance band · Knee-friendly · Rehab-oriented | Beginner |
| 4.11 | Step-Up | Compound · Dumbbell · Unilateral · Functional | Beginner → Intermediate |
| 4.12 | Dumbbell Squat (dumbbells at sides) | Compound · Dumbbell · Bilateral | Beginner → Intermediate |

## 4.1 Barbell Back Squat

**Category:** Compound · Barbell · Bilateral · **Level:** Intermediate → Advanced

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** Quadriceps, gluteus maximus, adductor magnus
- **Secondary:** Hamstrings (stabilisers), erector spinae, calves, core
- **Specific muscles:** Vastus lateralis, medialis, intermedius (rectus femoris less), glute max, adductor magnus
- **Joint actions:** Knee extension, hip extension, ankle dorsiflexion (descent) / plantarflexion

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Yes | Squat rack / power rack with safety pins | No | Yes | No | No | Bodyweight squat |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** Bar on upper traps (high bar) or rear delts (low bar), upper back tight, chest up.
- **Foot position:** Shoulder-width or slightly wider, toes out 15–30°.
- **Hands / grip:** Just outside shoulders, wrists straight, elbows under/behind bar.
- **Machine / setup adjustments:** J-hooks at mid-chest; safety pins just below bottom depth.
- **Starting range:** Standing tall after a two-step walkout.

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Unrack, two to three steps back, feet set.
2. **Initiate.** Big breath into the belly, brace, then break at hips and knees together.
3. **Movement path.** Sit down between the hips; knees travel forward and out over the toes; bar stays over mid-foot.
4. **Breathing.** Hold the breath down and through the hardest part; exhale near the top.
5. **Contraction.** Drive up through the whole foot, keeping the chest and hips rising together.
6. **End position.** Stand tall with hips and knees locked.
7. **Return.** Re-brace before the next rep.
8. **Recommended ROM.** As deep as you can keep a neutral spine and heels down — usually at or below parallel.

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** Knees in line with toes; bar path vertical over mid-foot; neutral spine.
- **Tempo:** 2–3 s down, controlled bounce-free bottom, drive up.
- **Muscle engagement:** Knees moving past toes is normal and safe for healthy knees; restricting it shifts stress to the hips and lower back (Fry et al., 2003).

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Knees caving in (valgus) | Cue 'knees out'; lighter load; strengthen glute medius |
| Heels lifting | Improve ankle mobility or use heel-elevated shoes/plates |
| Hips shooting up first ('good-morning squat') | Lighter load, pause squats, stronger quads |
| Losing brace at the bottom | Breathe and brace before every rep; consider a belt for heavy sets |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Learn with goblet squat first; then 3 × 8–10 | 3–4 × 6–10 | 3–5 × 3–8 (strength) or 6–12 (size) | 2–4 min | Add 2.5–5 kg when all sets hit the top of the range |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| High | Very high | Moderate (technical) | High | Good (rack + bar) | Safe with good technique and safeties set |

- **Advantages:** Trains the most muscle at once; best-studied lower-body exercise; deep squats grow glutes and adductors (Kubo et al., 2019)
- **Disadvantages:** Technically demanding; low back and mobility can limit it; rectus femoris grows little

**H. Alternatives**

Machine: Hack squat or Smith squat · Dumbbell: Goblet / dumbbell squat · Bodyweight: Bodyweight squat · Easier: Box squat, goblet squat · Harder: Pause squat, tempo squat, front squat

## 4.2 Barbell Front Squat

**Category:** Compound · Barbell · **Level:** Intermediate → Advanced

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** Quadriceps
- **Secondary:** Gluteus maximus, adductors, upper back, core
- **Specific muscles:** Vasti; glute max and adductor magnus (slightly less than back squat)
- **Joint actions:** Knee extension, hip extension

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Yes | Squat rack | No | Yes | No | No | Goblet squat |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** Bar on front delts touching the throat, elbows high, torso upright.
- **Foot position:** Shoulder-width, toes slightly out.
- **Hands / grip:** Clean grip (fingertips) or crossed arms; straps around the bar are an option.
- **Machine / setup adjustments:** Rack at mid-chest.
- **Starting range:** Standing.

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Step under the bar, set it on the front delts touching the throat, elbows high; walk out two steps.
2. **Initiate.** Brace hard (big belly breath), then break at the knees and hips together.
3. **Movement path.** Sit straight down between the heels; torso stays upright; knees travel forward over the toes.
4. **Breathing.** Hold the breath through the descent and the bottom; exhale near the top.
5. **Contraction.** Drive up leading with the elbows so the chest stays tall and the bar doesn't roll forward.
6. **End position.** Stand tall with hips and knees extended.
7. **Return.** Re-brace before each rep.
8. **Recommended ROM.** As deep as an upright torso allows — usually below parallel.

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** Elbows high so the bar doesn't roll forward.
- **Tempo:** 2–3 s down, no bounce, drive up.
- **Muscle engagement:** More upright torso means less hip and spinal load and relatively more knee demand.

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Elbows dropping | Cue 'elbows up'; improve wrist/lat mobility or use straps |
| Rounding upper back | Lighter load; upper-back work |
| Heels lifting | Weightlifting shoes or a small heel raise; ankle mobility |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Learn with goblet squat | 3 × 5–8 | 3–5 × 3–8 | 2–3 min | Small load increases |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| High | High | Fair | High | Good | Safe; bar is easy to dump forward |

- **Advantages:** Quad-dominant, lower spinal load, good for upright posture
- **Disadvantages:** Rack position uncomfortable; lower loads than back squat

**H. Alternatives**

Machine: Hack squat · Dumbbell: Goblet squat · Bodyweight: Bodyweight squat · Easier: Goblet squat · Harder: Pause front squat

**I. Visual learning**

|---|---|

## 4.3 Goblet Squat

**Category:** Compound · Dumbbell/Kettlebell · Bilateral · **Level:** Beginner

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** Quadriceps, gluteus maximus
- **Secondary:** Adductors, core, upper back
- **Specific muscles:** Vasti, glute max, adductor magnus
- **Joint actions:** Knee and hip extension; ankle dorsiflexion

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| No | — | Yes (or kettlebell) | No | No | No | Bodyweight squat |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** Dumbbell held vertically at the chest, elbows down, torso upright.
- **Foot position:** Shoulder-width, toes out 15–30°.
- **Hands / grip:** Cup the top head of the dumbbell.
- **Starting range:** Standing.

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Hold the weight at the sternum.
2. **Initiate.** Brace and sit down between the heels.
3. **Movement path.** Elbows track inside the knees; torso upright.
4. **Breathing.** Inhale down, exhale up.
5. **Contraction.** Push the floor away with the whole foot.
6. **End position.** Stand tall.
7. **Return.** Lower again under control.
8. **Recommended ROM.** As deep as heels-down allows (elbows to knees).

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** Knees out over toes.
- **Tempo:** 3 s down, 1 s pause, up.
- **Muscle engagement:** The front load naturally teaches an upright, balanced squat.

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Dumbbell drifting away from chest | Keep it pinned to the sternum |
| Heels lifting | Small heel wedge; ankle mobility |
| Rounding at bottom | Stop just above the point of rounding |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2–3 × 10–12 | 3 × 10–15 heavier | Use as warm-up or high-rep finisher | 90 s | Heavier dumbbell → back squat |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Moderate–high | Moderate | Excellent | Moderate | Excellent | Very safe |

- **Advantages:** The best squat-teaching tool
- **Disadvantages:** Load is limited by how heavy you can hold

**H. Alternatives**

Machine: Leg press · Dumbbell: Dumbbell squat (sides) · Bodyweight: Bodyweight squat · Easier: Box goblet squat · Harder: Heel-elevated / pause goblet squat

**I. Visual learning**

| Item | Resource |
|---|---|
| Best tutorial (verified) | [Perfect Your Squat: Step-By-Step Form Guide](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8Kls95w2jFA) — *Squat University* |
| Video type / learning focus | Squat tutorial (same pattern). Bracing, depth and knee tracking apply directly to the goblet squat |
| Most suitable educators | Squat University, Dan John-style coaches |

## 4.4 Hack Squat (machine)

**Category:** Compound · Machine · **Level:** Beginner → Advanced

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** Quadriceps
- **Secondary:** Gluteus maximus, adductors
- **Specific muscles:** Vastus lateralis, medialis, intermedius
- **Joint actions:** Knee extension, hip extension

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Yes | Hack squat machine (45° sled, shoulder pads) | No | No | No | No | Heel-elevated bodyweight squat |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** Back and hips against the pad, shoulders under pads.
- **Foot position:** Shoulder-width, mid-platform; lower placement = more knee bend (quad bias).
- **Hands / grip:** On handles.
- **Machine / setup adjustments:** Release the safety handles; set safety stops at the bottom depth.
- **Starting range:** Legs straight but not locked.

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Unlock safeties, stand straight.
2. **Initiate.** Bend the knees and hips together.
3. **Movement path.** Descend along the sled path; knees travel forward over toes.
4. **Breathing.** Inhale down, brace.
5. **Contraction.** Drive through the mid-foot.
6. **End position.** Stop just short of lockout to keep tension, or lock out briefly.
7. **Return.** Control each descent.
8. **Recommended ROM.** Deep — thighs at least parallel to the platform.

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** Knees track toes; hips stay on the pad.
- **Tempo:** 2–3 s down, no bounce.
- **Muscle engagement:** Machine stability lets you push close to failure safely.

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Hips peeling off the pad at bottom | Reduce depth slightly or adjust feet |
| Bouncing | Pause briefly at bottom |
| Partial reps | Full depth before adding weight |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2–3 × 10–12 | 3–4 × 8–12 | 3–5 × 6–15 incl. near-failure sets | 2–3 min | Reps then plates |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| High | High | Excellent | Low | Moderate (not every gym) | Very safe |

- **Advantages:** Heavy, deep quad loading without balance or spinal-bracing limits
- **Disadvantages:** Machine-dependent; less carryover to free-weight squat skill

**H. Alternatives**

Machine: Leg press, pendulum squat · Dumbbell: Heel-elevated goblet squat · Bodyweight: Heel-elevated bodyweight squat · Easier: Leg press · Harder: Reverse hack squat, pause hack

**I. Visual learning**

## 4.5 45-Degree Leg Press

**Category:** Compound · Machine · **Level:** Beginner → Advanced

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** Quadriceps
- **Secondary:** Gluteus maximus, adductors
- **Specific muscles:** Vasti; glute max and adductor magnus more with deep range and higher foot placement
- **Joint actions:** Knee extension, hip extension

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Yes | 45° plate-loaded leg press (incline sled) | No | No | No | No | Bodyweight squat |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** Back flat on the pad, hips fully in the seat.
- **Foot position:** Shoulder-width, middle of platform. Lower = more quads; higher = more glutes/hamstrings.
- **Hands / grip:** On side handles.
- **Machine / setup adjustments:** Seat back angle so the low back stays flat at the bottom; set the safety catches.
- **Starting range:** Legs extended, safeties released.

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Push the sled up, release the safety catches.
2. **Initiate.** Lower by bending the knees toward the chest.
3. **Movement path.** Knees track over toes; feet flat.
4. **Breathing.** Inhale down.
5. **Contraction.** Press through the whole foot.
6. **End position.** Knees nearly straight — don't snap them locked.
7. **Return.** Lower under control.
8. **Recommended ROM.** As deep as the low back stays on the pad (tailbone must not curl up).

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** Feet flat, knees in line with toes.
- **Tempo:** 2–3 s down, 1 s up.
- **Muscle engagement:** Foot position changes hip vs knee range, not 'inner vs outer quad' (da Silva et al., 2008).

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Lower back/tailbone lifting off the pad | Reduce depth or adjust seat |
| Locking knees hard | Stop just short of lockout |
| Heels lifting | Move feet higher |
| Ego loading with short range | Full range first, then load |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2–3 × 10–15 | 3–4 × 8–15 | 3–5 × 6–20 | 2–3 min | Reps then plates; single-leg for imbalances |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| High | High (machine-specific) | Excellent | Very low | Excellent | Very safe when depth is controlled |

- **Advantages:** Easy to learn, easy to overload, minimal technique and spinal load
- **Disadvantages:** Less core/balance demand; strength carries over modestly to free weights

**H. Alternatives**

Machine: Hack squat, horizontal leg press · Dumbbell: Goblet squat · Bodyweight: Bodyweight squat · Easier: Horizontal leg press · Harder: Single-leg leg press, pause reps

**I. Visual learning**

## 4.6 Leg Extension

**Category:** Isolation · Machine · **Level:** Beginner → Advanced

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** Quadriceps (all four heads, especially rectus femoris)
- **Secondary:** —
- **Specific muscles:** Rectus femoris, vastus lateralis, medialis, intermedius
- **Joint actions:** Knee extension only

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Yes | Seated leg extension machine | No | No | Alternative (ankle cuff) | Alternative | Reverse Nordic / sissy squat |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** Seated, back on pad; leaning back slightly lengthens rectus femoris.
- **Foot position:** Shin pad on the lower shin just above the ankle.
- **Hands / grip:** Hold the handles to keep hips down.
- **Machine / setup adjustments:** Knee joint aligned with the machine's pivot point; backrest so the knee is at the seat edge.
- **Starting range:** Knees bent ~90° or more.

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Align knee with the pivot.
2. **Initiate.** Extend the knees by contracting the quads.
3. **Movement path.** Lift the pad in a smooth arc.
4. **Breathing.** Exhale up.
5. **Contraction.** Squeeze 1 s at full extension.
6. **End position.** Legs straight.
7. **Return.** Lower slowly over 2–3 s.
8. **Recommended ROM.** Full range; the deep-bent (lengthened) position is especially valuable.

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** Hips stay in the seat.
- **Tempo:** 1 s up, 1 s hold, 3 s down.
- **Muscle engagement:** In Kubo et al. (2019) squat training grew the vasti and adductors but did not significantly grow the rectus femoris; knee-extension training is the main way to grow it.

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Kicking/momentum | Slow the eccentric |
| Pivot misaligned | Adjust backrest |
| Hips lifting | Grip handles; lower load |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2–3 × 12–15 | 3 × 10–15 | 3–4 × 8–20, lengthened partials | 60–90 s | Reps then plates |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| High (esp. rectus femoris) | Moderate | Excellent | Very low | Excellent | Safe for healthy knees; adjust range if painful |

- **Advantages:** Unique rectus-femoris stimulus; precise loading; rehab-friendly
- **Disadvantages:** No hip extension; open-chain only

**H. Alternatives**

Machine: — · Dumbbell: — · Bodyweight: Reverse Nordic, sissy squat · Easier: Single-leg, light · Harder: Partials in the stretched range, slow eccentrics

## 4.7 Bulgarian Split Squat

**Category:** Compound · Dumbbell/Barbell · Unilateral · **Level:** Intermediate

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** Quadriceps, gluteus maximus
- **Secondary:** Adductors, hamstrings, gluteus medius (stability)
- **Specific muscles:** Vasti, glute max (lengthened), adductor magnus, glute medius
- **Joint actions:** Knee and hip extension on the front leg; hip-flexor stretch on the rear leg

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Optional | Smith machine (optional) | Yes | Optional | No | No | Bodyweight split squat |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** Rear foot laces-down on a bench ~knee height.
- **Foot position:** Front foot about one long stride ahead; short stance + upright torso = quads; long stance + forward lean = glutes.
- **Hands / grip:** Dumbbells at sides.
- **Starting range:** Standing on the front leg.

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Set the stance, weight mostly on the front foot.
2. **Initiate.** Lower straight down by bending the front knee.
3. **Movement path.** Knee travels forward over the toes; rear knee drops toward the floor.
4. **Breathing.** Inhale down, exhale up.
5. **Contraction.** Drive through the front mid-foot.
6. **End position.** Stand on the front leg, hip and knee extended.
7. **Return.** Lower again; finish all reps then switch.
8. **Recommended ROM.** Rear knee near the floor; front thigh at or below parallel.

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** Front knee tracks toes; pelvis level.
- **Tempo:** 2–3 s down.
- **Muscle engagement:** Balance improves quickly; start with bodyweight.

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Stance too short or long | Adjust until the front heel stays down at the bottom |
| Pushing off the back foot | Back leg is only a kickstand |
| Knee caving | Lighter load; push knee out |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 × 8–10 / leg bodyweight | 3 × 8–12 / leg | 3–4 × 6–12 / leg heavy or deficit | 90 s between legs | Bodyweight → dumbbells → heavier; straps for grip |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| High | High | Fair (balance) | High | Excellent | Safe |

- **Advantages:** High glute and quad stimulus at long length; fixes left–right imbalances; low spinal load
- **Disadvantages:** Very fatiguing; balance limits load early on

**H. Alternatives**

Machine: Smith-machine split squat · Dumbbell: Dumbbell split squat · Bodyweight: Split squat · Easier: Static split squat (rear foot on floor) · Harder: Deficit BSS, barbell BSS

**I. Visual learning**

| Item | Resource |
|---|---|

## 4.8 Smith Machine Squat

**Category:** Compound · Machine · **Level:** Beginner → Intermediate

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** Quadriceps, gluteus maximus
- **Secondary:** Adductors
- **Specific muscles:** Vasti, glute max
- **Joint actions:** Knee and hip extension

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Yes | Smith machine (guided bar) | No | No | No | No | Bodyweight squat |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** Bar on upper traps, torso upright.
- **Foot position:** Slightly in front of the bar for quad bias and to suit the fixed bar path.
- **Hands / grip:** Just outside shoulders.
- **Machine / setup adjustments:** Set the safety stops just below bottom depth.
- **Starting range:** Standing, bar unhooked.

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Set the bar on the upper traps, feet slightly in front of the bar; unhook by rotating the bar.
2. **Initiate.** Brace and break at knees and hips together.
3. **Movement path.** Descend vertically; knees track over toes; back stays against the bar path.
4. **Breathing.** Breath held and braced through the descent.
5. **Contraction.** Drive through the whole foot.
6. **End position.** Stand tall, knees just short of hard lockout.
7. **Return.** Control each descent; re-hook the bar by rotating it at the end.
8. **Recommended ROM.** Full depth with heels down.

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** Feet placement decides comfort — experiment.
- **Tempo:** 2–3 s down, 1 s up.
- **Muscle engagement:** Fixed path removes balance demand.

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Feet directly under the bar | Step feet slightly forward |
| Not setting stops | Always set them |
| Low back rounding at depth | Reduce depth slightly or bring feet a little further forward |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2–3 × 10–12 | 3–4 × 8–12 | 3–4 × 6–12 | 2 min | Reps then load |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| High | Moderate | Good | Low | Good | Safe |

- **Advantages:** Stable, easy to push hard; also used for split squats
- **Disadvantages:** Fixed path may not suit everyone

**H. Alternatives**

Machine: Hack squat · Dumbbell: Goblet squat · Bodyweight: Bodyweight squat · Easier: Leg press · Harder: Pause Smith squat

**I. Visual learning**

| Item | Resource |
|---|---|
| Best tutorial (verified) | [The Best & Worst QUAD Exercises (Ranked Using Science)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kIXcoivzGf8) — *Jeff Nippard* |
| Video type / learning focus | Evidence ranking. Where machine squats sit among quad exercises |
