## 10.3 Single-Leg Balance Progression

**Category:** Stability · Bodyweight · Rehab-oriented · **Level:** Beginner → Advanced

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** Ankle stabilisers (fibularis, tibialis anterior/posterior)
- **Secondary:** Foot intrinsics, gluteus medius
- **Specific muscles:** Fibularis longus/brevis, tibialis posterior, abductor hallucis
- **Joint actions:** Isometric ankle/foot control in all directions

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| No | — | No | No | No | Optional (perturbation) | Is bodyweight |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** Standing on one leg, soft knee, hips level.
- **Foot position:** Tripod foot: heel, big-toe base, little-toe base.
- **Hands / grip:** Free (near support).
- **Starting range:** Single-leg stance.

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Stand on one leg.
2. **Initiate.** Keep the arch active, toes relaxed.
3. **Movement path.** Progress: eyes open → eyes closed → soft surface → reaching or catching a ball.
4. **Breathing.** Breathe normally.
5. **Contraction.** Small ankle corrections are the training effect.
6. **End position.** Hold.
7. **Return.** Switch legs.
8. **Recommended ROM.** 30–60 s per set.

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** Pelvis level.
- **Tempo:** Continuous holds of 30–60 s.
- **Muscle engagement:** Balance training reduced ankle-sprain recurrence in an RCT (Hupperets et al., 2009).

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Clawing the toes | Relax toes, lift arch |
| Hip dropping | Squeeze stance glute |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 3 × 30 s/leg eyes open | 3 × 30 s eyes closed | Unstable surface + perturbation | As needed | Harder conditions |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Low | Low | Excellent | High | Excellent | Very safe |

- **Advantages:** Proven ankle-sprain prevention; zero equipment
- **Disadvantages:** Not a strength builder

**H. Alternatives**

Machine: — · Dumbbell: Holding a DB · Bodyweight: This is BW · Easier: Hand on wall · Harder: Eyes closed on foam, reach drills

## 10.4 Knee-to-Wall Ankle Mobility (dorsiflexion)

**Category:** Mobility · **Level:** Beginner

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** Soleus and gastrocnemius (stretched)
- **Secondary:** Ankle joint capsule
- **Specific muscles:** Soleus, Achilles tendon
- **Joint actions:** Weight-bearing ankle dorsiflexion

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| No | — | Optional (on knee for load) | No | No | Optional (joint mobilisation) | Is bodyweight |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** Half-kneeling or standing facing a wall.
- **Foot position:** Front foot a few cm from the wall, heel down.
- **Hands / grip:** On the wall.
- **Starting range:** Knee behind the toes.

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Set foot.
2. **Initiate.** Drive the knee forward toward the wall over the 2nd–3rd toe.
3. **Movement path.** Keep the heel down.
4. **Breathing.** Breathe out as you push.
5. **Contraction.** Feel stretch in the lower calf/ankle.
6. **End position.** Knee touching the wall (or as far as possible).
7. **Return.** Return.
8. **Recommended ROM.** Move foot further back as mobility improves.

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** Knee over 2nd–3rd toe; arch doesn't collapse.
- **Tempo:** Slow 10 reps or 2–3 s holds.
- **Muscle engagement:** Roughly 10 cm toe-to-wall is often used as a reference for adequate dorsiflexion (clinical rule of thumb).

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Heel lifting | Stop at that point |
| Knee collapsing in | Aim knee over toes |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 × 10 / side | 2 × 10 + 3 × 20 s loaded holds | Before every squat session | None needed | Increase toe-to-wall distance; add load on the knee |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Not a hypertrophy exercise | Not a strength exercise | Excellent | Low | Excellent | Very safe |

- **Advantages:** Improves squat depth and ankle mechanics
- **Disadvantages:** Not strengthening

**H. Alternatives**

Machine: — · Dumbbell: Weighted knee-over-toe · Bodyweight: This is BW · Easier: Wall-supported · Harder: Banded mobilisation

# 11. Foot and toe exercise library

Ten intrinsic muscles and the long toe flexors and extensors shape the arch and control push-off. These exercises are low-load motor-control and strength drills; do them barefoot.

| # | Exercise | Category | Level |
|---|---|---|---|
| 11.1 | Short-Foot Exercise | Bodyweight · Stability · Rehab-oriented | Beginner |
| 11.2 | Toe Yoga (big toe / lesser toe dissociation) | Bodyweight · Motor control | Beginner |
| 11.3 | Toe Spreading (abduction) | Bodyweight · Isolation | Beginner |
| 11.4 | Towel Toe Curls | Bodyweight · Isolation | Beginner |
| 11.5 | Big-Toe Raise and Press | Bodyweight · Isolation | Beginner |

## 11.1 Short-Foot Exercise

**Category:** Bodyweight · Stability · Rehab-oriented · **Level:** Beginner

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** Abductor hallucis, flexor digitorum brevis
- **Secondary:** Quadratus plantae, interossei, tibialis posterior
- **Specific muscles:** Medial-arch intrinsic muscles
- **Joint actions:** Shortening the foot (arch lift) without curling the toes

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| No | — | No | No | No | No | Is bodyweight |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** Seated first, then standing, then single-leg.
- **Foot position:** Foot flat, toes relaxed.
- **Hands / grip:** —
- **Starting range:** Arch relaxed.

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Foot flat.
2. **Initiate.** Pull the big-toe base toward the heel without curling the toes.
3. **Movement path.** The arch rises ('doming').
4. **Breathing.** Breathe normally.
5. **Contraction.** Hold 5–10 s.
6. **End position.** Raised arch, toes long and flat.
7. **Return.** Relax.
8. **Recommended ROM.** Small movement — quality over size.

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** Toes stay flat on the floor.
- **Tempo:** 5–10 s holds.
- **Muscle engagement:** Short-foot training improved arch height and balance in small trials (Mulligan & Cook, 2013).

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Curling toes | Keep toes long |
| Rolling to outside of foot | Keep big-toe base down |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Seated 2 × 10 × 5 s | Standing 3 × 10 × 5–10 s | Single-leg or during squats/RDLs | Minimal | Seated → standing → single-leg → in movement |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Low | Low | Excellent | Low | Excellent | Very safe |

- **Advantages:** Activates arch muscles; carries over to all standing exercises
- **Disadvantages:** Feels awkward at first; small effect size

**H. Alternatives**

Machine: — · Dumbbell: — · Bodyweight: This is BW · Easier: Seated · Harder: Single-leg, during calf raises

## 11.2 Toe Yoga (big toe / lesser toe dissociation)

*Also called: lesser-toe extension/flexion drill.* 
**Category:** Bodyweight · Motor control · **Level:** Beginner

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** EHL/FHL (big toe), EDL/FDL and lumbricals (lesser toes)
- **Secondary:** Intrinsic foot muscles
- **Specific muscles:** Extensor/flexor hallucis longus and brevis, extensor/flexor digitorum, lumbricals
- **Joint actions:** Independent big-toe and lesser-toe flexion/extension

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| No | — | No | No | No | No | Is bodyweight |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** Seated, feet flat.
- **Foot position:** Hip-width.
- **Hands / grip:** —
- **Starting range:** Toes relaxed.

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Feet flat.
2. **Initiate.** Lift the big toe while pressing the other four down.
3. **Movement path.** Then reverse: press the big toe down and lift the other four.
4. **Breathing.** Breathe.
5. **Contraction.** Hold each position 3–5 s.
6. **End position.** Alternate.
7. **Return.** Relax.
8. **Recommended ROM.** 10 cycles.

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** Ankle and arch still.
- **Tempo:** Slow alternation.
- **Muscle engagement:** Trains toe control and dissociation; useful for balance and push-off mechanics.

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Whole foot moving | Smaller movement |
| Unable to separate | Use fingers to assist at first |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 × 10 cycles | 3 × 10 standing | Single-leg standing | None needed | Seated → standing → single-leg |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Not a hypertrophy exercise | Low (foot-specific) | Excellent | Low | Excellent | Very safe |

- **Advantages:** Improves toe control and awareness
- **Disadvantages:** Minimal strength effect

**H. Alternatives**

## 11.3 Toe Spreading (abduction)

**Category:** Bodyweight · Isolation · **Level:** Beginner

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** Abductor hallucis, abductor digiti minimi, dorsal interossei
- **Secondary:** —
- **Specific muscles:** Abductor hallucis, abductor digiti minimi, dorsal interossei
- **Joint actions:** Toe abduction

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| No | — | No | No | No | Optional (small band around toes) | Is bodyweight |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** Seated, feet flat.
- **Foot position:** Toes relaxed.
- **Hands / grip:** —
- **Starting range:** Toes together.

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Feet flat.
2. **Initiate.** Spread all toes as wide as possible.
3. **Movement path.** Keep them on the floor.
4. **Breathing.** Breathe.
5. **Contraction.** Hold 5 s.
6. **End position.** Maximum spread.
7. **Return.** Relax.
8. **Recommended ROM.** 10 reps of 5-second holds.

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** Toes flat.
- **Tempo:** 5 s holds.
- **Muscle engagement:** Wide toe base improves foot stability.

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Lifting toes | Keep them down |
| Curling the toes as you spread | Keep toes long and flat |
| Arch collapsing | Combine with a light short-foot |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 × 10 | 3 × 10 with band | Standing | None needed | Add band |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Not a hypertrophy exercise | Low (foot-specific) | Excellent | Low | Excellent | Very safe |

- **Advantages:** Trains a neglected function
- **Disadvantages:** Small effect

## 11.4 Towel Toe Curls

*Also called: toe flexion exercise.* 
**Category:** Bodyweight · Isolation · **Level:** Beginner

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** FDL, FDB, FHL
- **Secondary:** Quadratus plantae, lumbricals
- **Specific muscles:** Long and short toe flexors
- **Joint actions:** Toe flexion

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| No | — | Optional (weight on towel) | No | No | No | Is bodyweight |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** Seated, foot on a towel.
- **Foot position:** Heel on floor.
- **Hands / grip:** —
- **Starting range:** Toes extended.

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Foot on towel.
2. **Initiate.** Curl the toes to scrunch the towel.
3. **Movement path.** Pull it toward you.
4. **Breathing.** Breathe.
5. **Contraction.** Grip.
6. **End position.** Towel bunched.
7. **Return.** Release and reset.
8. **Recommended ROM.** Until the towel is fully gathered.

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** Heel stays down.
- **Tempo:** Steady, controlled scrunches; 1 s squeeze each.
- **Muscle engagement:** Basic toe-flexor strength; add a weight on the far end.

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Lifting heel | Keep heel down |
| Only the big toe working | Curl all five toes evenly |
| Cramping in the arch | Rest; shorter sets |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 × 1 towel length | 3 × with weight | Heavier | None needed | Add weight |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Not a hypertrophy exercise | Low (foot-specific) | Excellent | Low | Excellent | Very safe |

- **Advantages:** Simple toe-flexor work
- **Disadvantages:** Encourages clawing if overdone — pair with short-foot

## 11.5 Big-Toe Raise and Press

*Also called: big-toe extension and flexion.* 
**Category:** Bodyweight · Isolation · **Level:** Beginner

**A. Anatomy**

- **Primary:** EHL and EHB (raise); FHL and FHB (press)
- **Secondary:** Abductor hallucis
- **Specific muscles:** Big-toe extensors and flexors
- **Joint actions:** First MTP extension and flexion

**B. Equipment**

| Machine | Exact machine | Dumbbell | Barbell | Cable | Band | Bodyweight alternative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| No | — | No | No | No | Optional | Is bodyweight |

**C. Starting position**

- **Body posture:** Seated or standing.
- **Foot position:** Flat.
- **Hands / grip:** —
- **Starting range:** Neutral.

**D. Execution and form**

1. **Start.** Sit or stand with the foot flat and weight evenly on heel, big-toe base and little-toe base.
2. **Initiate.** Raise: lift only the big toe as high as possible while the other four stay on the floor.
3. **Movement path.** Press: put the big toe down and press it firmly into the floor (imagine trapping a card under it) while lifting the other four slightly.
4. **Breathing.** Breathe normally throughout.
5. **Contraction.** Hold each position 3–5 s.
6. **End position.** Big toe fully extended (raise) or pressed flat and firm (press), arch stays lifted.
7. **Return.** Relax between reps.
8. **Recommended ROM.** 10 raises and 10 presses per foot.

**E. Technique details**

- **Posture and alignment:** Ankle still.
- **Tempo:** 3–5 s holds per position.
- **Muscle engagement:** Big-toe press strength supports push-off; extension mobility supports the windlass mechanism.

| Common mistake | How to correct it |
|---|---|
| Rolling the foot | Keep it flat |
| Ankle rolling inward or outward | Keep the tripod contact |
| All toes moving together | Use a finger to hold the lesser toes down at first |

**F. Training parameters**

| Beginner | Intermediate | Advanced | Rest | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 × 10 | 3 × 10 standing | Single-leg | None needed | Standing → single-leg |

**G. Evaluation**

| Hypertrophy | Strength | Beginner suitability | Stability demand | Equipment access | Safety |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Not a hypertrophy exercise | Low (foot-specific) | Excellent | Low | Excellent | Very safe |

- **Advantages:** Targets big-toe function directly
- **Disadvantages:** Small effect

**H. Alternatives**

Machine: — · Dumbbell: — · Bodyweight: BW · Easier: Hand-assisted · Harder: Single-leg

**I. Visual learning**

| Item | Resource |
|---|---|
| Best tutorial | No direct video verified in this research; use the search link (prefer E3 Rehab, Squat University, Jeff Nippard or a physiotherapist channel in the results) |
| Most suitable educators | Physiotherapists |

## 12.25 Differences between similar machines

| Comparison | Key difference | Choose… |
|---|---|---|
| 45° leg press vs horizontal leg press | 45° sleds usually allow deeper range and heavier loading; horizontal versions are easier to enter and often selectorised | 45° for growth and strength; horizontal for beginners or rehab |
| Leg press vs hack squat | Hack squat keeps the torso more upright and follows a squat path with deeper knee flexion; leg press lets the hips flex more and is easier to load | Hack squat for quad emphasis; leg press for overall volume and comfort |
| Hack squat vs Smith squat | Hack is angled and back-supported; Smith is vertical and free-standing (more balance) | Hack for quads; Smith if no hack is available or for split squats |
| Seated vs lying vs standing leg curl | Seated keeps hamstrings longest (best evidence); lying is shorter; standing is unilateral and shortest | Seated first, lying second |
| Standing vs seated vs donkey calf raise | Straight-knee (standing, donkey) targets gastrocnemius; bent-knee (seated) targets soleus | One of each |
| Hip-thrust machine vs Glute Drive vs barbell | Same movement; machines remove setup time and bar discomfort | Whichever you will use consistently |
| Hip abduction vs cable abduction | Machine is seated (hip flexed), cable is standing (hip extended, more functional) | Machine for loading; cable/side-lying for variety |
| GHD vs Nordic bench | GHD allows full glute-ham raises and back extensions; Nordic bench only Nordics | GHD if available |
| Reverse hyper vs 45° back extension | Reverse hyper moves the legs (no spinal compression, traction at bottom); back extension moves the torso | Back extension for accessibility |
| Belt squat / assisted squat vs barbell squat | Load hangs from the hips — no spinal compression | When the back limits squatting |


# 13. Dumbbell and free-weight encyclopedia

**Best free-weight starter set for a beginner:** goblet squat, dumbbell RDL, reverse lunge, dumbbell glute bridge/hip thrust, single-leg calf raise and wall tibialis raise. Together they cover quads, glutes, hamstrings, adductors and calves with only a pair of dumbbells and a step.

**Additional exercises worth knowing** (folded into other entries to avoid redundancy): heel-elevated goblet squat (more quad, see 4.3 alternatives), deficit reverse lunge (more glute/quad range, 7.1), B-stance hip thrust (unilateral bias, 3.1), kettlebell deadlift (hinge teaching tool, 5.9 alternatives), and Cossack squat (adductor mobility and strength — [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=cossack+squat+tutorial)).

# 14. Exercise variations and biomechanics

## 14.1 Why variations produce different demands

| Factor | What it changes | Practical example |
|---|---|---|
| **Joint angle** | Which joint does more work and which muscles have the better leverage | A more upright torso (front squat, hack squat) increases knee demand; more forward lean (low-bar squat, long-stride lunge) increases hip demand |
| **Muscle length** | Two-joint muscles are lengthened or shortened by the *other* joint's position | Seated leg curl (hip flexed) keeps hamstrings long; the squat shortens rectus femoris at the hip while lengthening it at the knee, so it barely changes length |
| **Loading (resistance) profile** | Where in the range the exercise is hardest | Hip thrust is hardest at the top (short glute); deep squat and split squat are hardest near the bottom (long glute) |
| **Stability** | How much load you can use vs how much balance you must provide | Machines let you approach failure safely; free-weight single-leg work trains control but caps load |
| **Range of motion** | Total stretch and stimulus | Full and lengthened-range training is generally at least as good as partials at short lengths (Pedrosa 2022; Kassiano 2023) |
| **Difficulty** | Technique learning time, fatigue | Leg press < goblet squat < back squat; Nordic curl is very hard |
| **Equipment** | Access | Dumbbells everywhere; GHD, reverse hyper and pendulum squat are rare |

## 14.2 Quadriceps

| Exercise | Knee range | Hip demand | Stability | Distinctive feature |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Back squat | Deep | High | High | Most muscle and best strength carryover; small rectus femoris effect |
| Hack squat | Deep | Moderate | Low | Quad-biased, very stable |
| Leg press | Moderate–deep | Moderate | Very low | Easiest to overload safely |
| Leg extension | Full (open chain) | None | Very low | The only common exercise that loads rectus femoris strongly |
| Bulgarian split squat | Deep | High (front leg) | High | Unilateral, lengthened glute and quad, low spinal load |

**Interpretation.** A squat-pattern exercise *plus* a leg extension covers all four heads. Foot rotation (toes in vs out) does not meaningfully isolate the vastus medialis or lateralis — claims to the contrary rely on small, inconsistent EMG differences.

## 14.3 Hamstrings

| Exercise | Function trained | Hamstring length | Short head trained? | Distinctive feature |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Seated leg curl | Knee flexion | Long (hip flexed) | **Yes** | Best direct hypertrophy evidence (Maeo et al., 2021) |
| Lying leg curl | Knee flexion | Shorter (hip extended) | Yes | Good alternative |
| Romanian deadlift | Hip extension | Long at bottom | **No** (one-joint, knee only) | Heavy, also trains glutes/adductors |
| Nordic curl | Eccentric knee flexion | Moderate–long | Yes | Injury-prevention evidence; very hard |
| Glute-ham raise | Both | Moderate | Yes | Needs GHD |

**Interpretation.** Hamstrings need **both** a hinge and a curl. Hinges alone leave the biceps femoris short head and much of the semitendinosus under-trained.

## 14.4 Glutes

| Exercise | Glute length when hardest | Load potential | Distinctive feature |
|---|---|---|---|
| Hip thrust | Short | Very high | Peak contraction; low knee/spinal stress |
| Glute bridge | Short | Low–moderate | Bodyweight entry point |
| Cable kickback | Short–moderate | Low | Low-fatigue isolation |
| Hip abduction | Varies | Moderate | Targets glute medius/minimus and upper glute max |
| Bulgarian split squat | Long | High | Lengthened glute stimulus, single-leg control |

**Interpretation.** Hip thrusts and squats produced similar glute growth in untrained people (Plotkin et al., 2023), but deeper squats grow the glutes more than shallow ones (Kubo et al., 2019). A practical glute plan uses one long-length movement (deep squat, split squat or RDL) plus one short-length movement (hip thrust) plus abduction.

## 14.5 Calves

| Exercise | Knee | Main target | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| Standing calf raise | Straight | Gastrocnemius (+ soleus) | Pause in the stretch |
| Seated calf raise | Bent 90° | Soleus | Gastrocnemius slackened |
| Donkey calf raise | Straight | Gastrocnemius | Comfortable deep stretch |
| Single-leg calf raise | Straight | Both + stabilisers | Needs only a step |

**Interpretation.** Train both a straight-knee and a bent-knee variation, always through a full range with a pause in the stretched position.

## 14.6 Ankles

| Exercise | Action | Main muscles |
|---|---|---|
| Tibialis raise | Dorsiflexion | Tibialis anterior, EHL, EDL |
| Reverse calf raise (= standing tibialis raise, §9.1) / banded dorsiflexion (§9.2) | Dorsiflexion | Tibialis anterior |
| Banded inversion | Inversion | Tibialis posterior, tibialis anterior |
| Banded eversion | Eversion | Fibularis longus, brevis |
| Calf raise (plantarflexion) and tibialis raise (dorsiflexion) | Both sagittal directions | Calves, shin |

## 14.7 Feet and toes

| Exercise | Function | Main muscles |
|---|---|---|
| Short-foot | Arch lift without toe curl | Abductor hallucis, FDB, quadratus plantae |
| Toe yoga | Big-toe vs lesser-toe dissociation | EHL/FHL, EDL/FDL, lumbricals |
| Toe spreading | Toe abduction | Abductor hallucis, abductor digiti minimi, dorsal interossei |
| Toe curls | Toe flexion | FDL, FDB, FHL |
| Big-toe raises/press | First-toe extension/flexion | EHL/EHB, FHL/FHB |

# 15. Effectiveness rankings

The rankings use categories, not scores, to avoid false precision. Reasoning is given for each "Essential" choice.

## 15.1 Quadriceps

| Category | Exercises | Reasoning |
|---|---|---|
| **Essential** | One of: hack squat, leg press, back squat · plus leg extension | A heavy squat pattern trains the vasti; the leg extension is the only practical way to grow the rectus femoris fully |
| **Highly recommended** | Bulgarian split squat | Unilateral, lengthened, low spinal load |
| **Useful alternatives** | Front squat, Smith squat, goblet squat, step-up, walking lunge | Context-dependent substitutes |
| **Optional accessories** | Sissy squat / reverse Nordic | Extra rectus-femoris work at long length |
| **Specialised** | Spanish squat, TKE | Knee pain and rehab |

## 15.2 Hamstrings

| Category | Exercises | Reasoning |
|---|---|---|
| **Essential** | Seated leg curl · Romanian deadlift | Both functions; seated curl has the best direct growth evidence |
| **Highly recommended** | Lying leg curl, Nordic curl | Substitute curl; eccentric strength and injury-prevention evidence |
| **Useful alternatives** | DB RDL, single-leg RDL, stiff-leg DL, 45° back extension | Equipment/skill substitutes |
| **Optional** | Glute-ham raise | Excellent but needs a GHD |
| **Specialised** | Conventional deadlift | Strength sport, not primarily leg hypertrophy |

## 15.3 Glutes and hip

| Category | Exercises | Reasoning |
|---|---|---|
| **Essential** | A deep squat or split squat (from 15.1) · hip abduction (machine or side-lying) | Long-length glute max stimulus plus direct glute med/min work |
| **Highly recommended** | Hip thrust, RDL | Short-length peak loading; long-length hinge |
| **Useful alternatives** | Glute bridge, cable kickback, 45° back extension, step-up | Simpler or lower-fatigue choices |
| **Optional** | Reverse hyperextension, kettlebell swing, hip-flexion work | Specific goals (power, hip flexor strength) |
| **Specialised** | Hip rotation series | Deep rotators and hip control |

## 15.4 Adductors

| Category | Exercises | Reasoning |
|---|---|---|
| **Essential** | Adduction machine *or* Copenhagen plank | Direct work; Copenhagen has injury-prevention evidence |
| **Highly recommended** | Deep squat / sumo squat | Adductor magnus is a major extensor in deep squats |
| **Useful alternatives** | Cable adduction | Standing, hip-extended position |

## 15.5 Calves, shins, ankles and feet

| Category | Exercises | Reasoning |
|---|---|---|
| **Essential** | Standing (or leg-press) calf raise · seated calf raise | Gastrocnemius and soleus both need loading |
| **Highly recommended** | Tibialis raise · single-leg balance | Shin strength; balance training has sprain-prevention evidence |
| **Useful alternatives** | Single-leg calf raise, donkey calf raise | Equipment substitutes |
| **Optional accessories** | Banded inversion/eversion, tib-post heel raise | Ankle stability and arch support |
| **Specialised** | Short-foot, toe yoga, toe spreading, toe curls, big-toe press | Foot control; low cost, modest evidence |

## 15.6 Nine-criterion assessment tables

Ratings are qualitative: **H** = high, **M** = moderate, **L** = low. For *stability demand* and *equipment*, read the column header literally ("Equipment availability H" = available in almost any gym; "Stability demand H" = hard to balance). For *safety*, H = low injury risk when done as described. These are organised judgements from the evidence and biomechanics above, not measurements.

# 16. Beginner-to-advanced progression

| Stage | Duration (typical) | Focus | Exercise types | Move up when… |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **1. Beginner** | 0–6 months | Learn patterns, controlled tempo, stable machines | Leg press, goblet squat, leg extension, seated/lying curl, DB RDL, glute bridge, hip abduction, calf machine, tibialis raise, short-foot, balance | You can perform every exercise with consistent form for 3 × 12 at a weight that feels hard (≈2 reps in reserve), and you've added load steadily for 8–12 weeks |
| **2. Intermediate** | 6–24 months | Dumbbells, unilateral work, progressive overload, targeted isolation | Hack squat or back squat, BSS, walking lunge, barbell RDL, hip thrust, Copenhagen, single-leg calf raise, banded ankle work | You squat/press and hinge with stable technique near failure, progress is slowing week to week, and you can manage 12–20 hard sets per muscle per week |
| **3. Advanced** | 2+ years | Heavier loads, specialised variations, periodised blocks | Front/pause squats, deficit BSS, Nordic and GHR, single-leg RDL heavy, lengthened partials, weighted reverse Nordic, tib bar | Progress requires planned blocks (e.g., 4–6 weeks of accumulation, then a deload) |

**Progression rules (all stages).** Use double progression: work within a rep range (e.g., 8–12); when all sets reach the top, add the smallest load step. Train most sets within 0–3 reps of failure. Deload (halve the sets) every 6–10 weeks or when performance falls two sessions running.

# 17. Pre-workout warm-up (10–15 minutes)

| Phase | Movement | Duration / reps | Purpose | Video |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **General** (5 min) | Treadmill walk (incline) or stationary bike | 5 min, easy-moderate | Raise tissue temperature; warm-ups improve subsequent performance in most studies (Fradkin et al., 2010) | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=incline+treadmill+walk+warm+up) |
| **Dynamic mobility** (3–4 min) | Leg swings, front–back | 10 / leg | Hip flexion/extension range | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=leg+swings+warm+up) |
| | Leg swings, side–side | 10 / leg | Adductor/abductor range | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=lateral+leg+swings+warm+up) |
| | Hip circles (standing or quadruped) | 5 / direction / leg | Hip rotation | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=standing+hip+circles+mobility) |
| | Ankle circles + knee-to-wall rocks | 10 / side | Ankle dorsiflexion | [How to Stretch Your Ankles — Squat University](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7hrTiJIqvr4) |
| | World's greatest stretch | 3 / side | Hip flexors, adductors, thoracic spine | [Squat University](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-CiWQ2IvY34) |
| | Walking lunges (bodyweight) | 5 / leg | Pattern rehearsal | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=bodyweight+walking+lunge+form) |
| | Dynamic hamstring sweeps / inchworms | 8 | Hamstring range | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=inchworm+hamstring+warm+up) |
| **Activation** (3 min) | Glute bridge | 10 + 5 s hold | Glute max | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=glute+bridge+activation) |
| | Banded lateral walk | 10 steps / direction | Glute medius | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=banded+lateral+walk+glute+activation) |
| | Bodyweight calf raises | 15 | Calves, Achilles | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=bodyweight+calf+raise) |
| | Tibialis raises | 15 | Shins | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=wall+tibialis+raise) |
| | Bodyweight squats | 10 | Full pattern | [The BEST Squat Warm-Up Tutorial — Squat University](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=t1e_6Yr0vdU) |
| **Exercise-specific** (3–5 min) | Warm-up sets of the first exercise | Bar/empty sled × 10; 50% × 6–8; 70% × 3–5; 85% × 1–2 | Prepare load, groove technique | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=warm+up+sets+ramp+up+squat) |

Long static stretches (>60 s per muscle) right before heavy lifting can slightly reduce force; short holds have trivial effects (Behm et al., 2016). Static stretching is placed in the cooldown.

# 18. Post-workout cooldown (8–12 minutes)

| Stretch | Target muscles | Execution | Duration | Precautions | Video |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Standing or side-lying quad stretch | Quadriceps, esp. rectus femoris | Heel to glute, knees together, pelvis tucked | 30–45 s / side | Don't arch the back; hold a support | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=standing+quad+stretch) |
| Supine or standing hamstring stretch | Hamstrings | Straight leg up (strap) or hinge with flat back | 30–45 s / side | Mild tension only; no nerve "zing" | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=hamstring+stretch+strap+physical+therapist) |
| Half-kneeling hip-flexor stretch | Iliopsoas, rectus femoris | Tuck pelvis, squeeze back glute, shift forward | 30–45 s / side | Pad under the knee | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=half+kneeling+hip+flexor+stretch) |
| Glute stretch (supine knee-to-opposite-shoulder) | Glute max, piriformis | Pull knee across body | 30 s / side | Stop if groin pinches | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=supine+glute+stretch+knee+to+shoulder) |
| Figure-four stretch | Glutes, deep rotators | Ankle on opposite knee, pull legs in | 30–45 s / side | Flex the crossed foot to protect the knee | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=figure+four+stretch) |
| Adductor stretch (butterfly or frog) | Adductors | Knees out, gentle forward lean | 30–45 s | Don't force the knees down | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=butterfly+adductor+stretch) |
| Gastrocnemius wall stretch | Gastrocnemius | Back leg straight, heel down | 30 s / side | Keep foot pointing forward | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=gastrocnemius+wall+stretch) |
| Soleus wall stretch | Soleus | Same but back knee bent | 30 s / side | Heel down | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=soleus+stretch+bent+knee) |
| Ankle circles / knee-to-wall rocks | Ankle joint | Slow circles; rocks | 10 each / side | Pain-free range | [How to Stretch Your Ankles — Squat University](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7hrTiJIqvr4) |
| Toe mobility (big-toe extension + toe spreading) | Toe flexors, plantar fascia | Kneel with toes tucked under (gently) or pull big toe back by hand | 30–60 s | Ease off if the forefoot or big toe hurts | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=big+toe+mobility+stretch) |

Video demonstrations: [hip-flexor stretch](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=half+kneeling+hip+flexor+stretch+physical+therapist) · [figure four stretch](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=figure+four+stretch) · [calf and soleus stretch](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=gastrocnemius+soleus+wall+stretch) · [toe mobility](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=big+toe+mobility+exercises) · [World's Greatest Stretch — Squat University](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-CiWQ2IvY34).

# 19. Complete lower-body muscle coverage matrix

Coverage key: **Direct** = a dedicated exercise loads it as a prime mover. **Indirect** = loaded meaningfully by compounds but not maximally. **Functional** = works mainly as a stabiliser. **Gap** = commonly under-trained in conventional leg days and needs dedicated work.

| Muscle | Direct exercise 1 | Direct exercise 2 | Compound involvement | Equipment | Coverage in a typical leg day | Coverage with this plan |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Gluteus maximus | Hip thrust | Bulgarian split squat | Squat, RDL, lunges | Barbell, DB | Direct | Direct |
| Gluteus medius | Hip abduction | Side-lying abduction | Single-leg work (functional) | Machine, BW | **Gap** | Direct |
| Gluteus minimus | Hip abduction | 90/90 internal rotation | Single-leg (functional) | Machine, BW | **Gap** | Direct |
| TFL | Hip abduction | Cable hip flexion | — | Machine, cable | Indirect | Indirect |
| Iliopsoas | Cable hip flexion / psoas march | Hanging knee raise | Lunges (stretch only) | Cable, band | **Gap** | Direct |
| Piriformis & deep five (obturators, gemelli, quadratus femoris) | 90/90 lifts | Banded clamshell | Squat (functional) | Band, BW | **Gap** | Direct (low load) |
| Psoas minor (absent in many people) | — | — | Trunk flexion (minor) | — | Not trainable separately | Not trainable separately |
| Sartorius | Cable hip flexion | Seated hip abduction | Leg curls (knee flexion) | Cable, machine | Indirect | Indirect (no dedicated work needed) |
| Rectus femoris | Leg extension | Reverse Nordic | Squat (minor) | Machine, BW | Indirect | Direct |
| Vastus lateralis | Hack squat / leg press | Leg extension | All squats/lunges | Machine | Direct | Direct |
| Vastus medialis | Hack squat / leg press | Leg extension | All squats/lunges | Machine | Direct | Direct |
| Vastus intermedius | Hack squat / leg press | Leg extension | All squats/lunges | Machine | Direct | Direct |
| Articularis genus | Any knee extension | — | Squat, leg press | — | Functional | Functional |
| Biceps femoris long head | Seated leg curl | RDL | GHR, Nordic | Machine, barbell | Direct | Direct |
| Biceps femoris short head | Seated leg curl | Nordic curl | **None** (one-joint) | Machine | **Gap** if only hinges are used | Direct |
| Semitendinosus | Seated leg curl | Nordic curl | RDL | Machine | Indirect | Direct |
| Semimembranosus | RDL | Seated leg curl | Squat (minor) | Barbell, machine | Direct | Direct |
| Adductor magnus | Deep squat | Adduction machine | RDL, sumo squat | Barbell, machine | Indirect | Direct |
| Adductor longus | Adduction machine | Copenhagen plank | Squat (minor) | Machine, BW | **Gap** | Direct |
| Adductor brevis | Adduction machine | Copenhagen plank | — | Machine | **Gap** | Direct |
| Gracilis | Copenhagen (long lever) | Adduction machine | Leg curls (minor) | BW, machine | **Gap** | Direct |
| Pectineus | Adduction machine | Cable hip flexion | — | Machine, cable | **Gap** | Indirect |
| Gastrocnemius (medial/lateral) | Standing calf raise | Leg-press calf raise | Walking/lunges (functional) | Machine | Often under-trained | Direct |
| Soleus | Seated calf raise | Bent-knee calf raise | Squat (functional) | Machine | **Gap** | Direct |
| Plantaris | Calf raises | — | — | — | Indirect | Indirect (negligible muscle) |
| Popliteus | Leg curls (initiation) | — | Squat lockout/unlock | Machine | Functional | Functional (no dedicated work needed) |
| Tibialis anterior | Tibialis raise | Banded dorsiflexion | Squat descent (functional) | BW, band | **Gap** | Direct |
| Tibialis posterior | Ball-squeeze heel raise | Banded inversion | Calf raises, balance | BW, band | **Gap** | Direct |
| Fibularis longus | Banded eversion | Single-leg balance | Calf raises (big-toe pressure) | Band | **Gap** | Direct |
| Fibularis brevis | Banded eversion | Single-leg balance | — | Band | **Gap** | Direct |
| Fibularis tertius | Tibialis raise | Banded eversion | — | BW, band | **Gap** | Indirect |
| Extensor digitorum longus | Tibialis raise | Toe raises | — | BW | **Gap** | Direct |
| Flexor digitorum longus | Toe curls | Calf raises | Push-off | BW | **Gap** | Direct |
| Extensor hallucis longus | Big-toe raise | Tibialis raise | — | BW | **Gap** | Direct |
| Flexor hallucis longus | Big-toe press | Calf raise through big toe | Push-off | BW | **Gap** | Direct |
| Abductor hallucis | Short-foot | Toe spreading | Balance (functional) | BW | **Gap** | Direct (low load) |
| Flexor digitorum brevis | Short-foot | Toe curls | — | BW | **Gap** | Direct (low load) |
| Abductor digiti minimi | Toe spreading | — | Balance | BW | **Gap** | Direct (low load) |
| Quadratus plantae | Toe curls | — | — | BW | **Gap** | Indirect |
| Lumbricals | Toe yoga | Toe press holds | — | BW | **Gap** | Direct (low load) |
| Interossei | Toe spreading / closing | — | — | BW | **Gap** | Direct (low load) |
| Flexor hallucis brevis | Big-toe press | — | Push-off | BW | **Gap** | Direct (low load) |
| Flexor digiti minimi brevis | Toe curls | Toe spreading/closing | Push-off | BW | **Gap** | Indirect |
| Adductor hallucis | Toe closing / short-foot | — | — | BW | **Gap** | Indirect |
| Extensor digitorum brevis | Lesser-toe raises (toe yoga) | — | — | BW | **Gap** | Indirect |
| Extensor hallucis brevis | Big-toe raise | — | — | BW | **Gap** | Direct (low load) |

**Main gaps in conventional leg training** (squat + leg press + leg extension + leg curl + calf raise): glute medius/minimus, deep hip rotators, iliopsoas, short adductors and gracilis, soleus, the entire shin and ankle group (tibialis anterior/posterior, fibularis), and every intrinsic foot muscle. Section 20 closes these with an accessory session.

# 20. Complete lower-body workout programs

**Must-do vs optional.** In every program, exercises marked **[M]** are must-do; **[O]** are optional supplements to add when time and recovery allow.

## 20.1 A. Beginner single-day leg routine (≈50–60 min, 1–2× per week)

| # | Exercise | Target | Equipment | Sets × reps | Rest | Difficulty | Video |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **[M]** 45° leg press | Quads, glutes | Machine | 3 × 10–12 | 2 min | Easy | [Leg press mistakes](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4eTMx8Epkks) |
| 2 | **[M]** Dumbbell RDL | Hamstrings, glutes | DB | 3 × 10 | 2 min | Moderate | [RDL tutorial](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5bJEigM5iVg) |
| 3 | **[M]** Seated leg curl | Hamstrings | Machine | 3 × 10–12 | 90 s | Easy | [Hamstring tips](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kXnRqoW6Alw) |
| 4 | **[M]** Leg extension | Quads (rectus femoris) | Machine | 2–3 × 12–15 | 90 s | Easy | [Leg extensions](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_95-vz83X4M) |
| 5 | **[M]** Hip abduction machine | Glute med/min | Machine | 2 × 15–20 | 60 s | Easy | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=hip+abduction+machine+form) |
| 6 | **[M]** Standing calf raise | Gastrocnemius | Machine | 3 × 12–15 | 60 s | Easy | [Calves](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-qsRtp_PbVM) |
| 7 | **[O]** Goblet squat | Quads, glutes | DB | 2 × 10 | 90 s | Easy | [Squat form guide](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8Kls95w2jFA) |
| 8 | **[O]** Tibialis raise | Shins | Wall | 2 × 15–20 | 45 s | Easy | [Shin raises](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=t3JPQkBMjuU) |

**Progression:** add reps to the top of each range, then add the smallest load step. **Recovery:** 48–72 h between sessions; mild soreness is normal, joint pain is not.

## 20.2 B. Comprehensive lower-body routine (≈70–80 min, 1× per week, intermediate)

| # | Exercise | Target | Equipment | Sets × reps | Rest | Difficulty | Video |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **[M]** Hack squat or back squat | Quads, glutes, adductors | Machine/barbell | 3–4 × 6–10 | 2–3 min | Moderate–hard | [Squat form guide](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8Kls95w2jFA) |
| 2 | **[M]** Romanian deadlift | Hamstrings, glutes | Barbell | 3 × 6–10 | 2–3 min | Moderate | [RDL tutorial](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5bJEigM5iVg) |
| 3 | **[M]** Bulgarian split squat | Quads, glutes | DB | 3 × 8–12 / leg | 90 s | Hard | [BSS tutorial](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hiLF_pF3EJM) |
| 4 | **[M]** Seated leg curl | Hamstrings | Machine | 3 × 8–12 | 90 s | Easy | [Leg curl setup](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=S367qaHeYWU) |
| 5 | **[M]** Leg extension | Quads | Machine | 3 × 10–15 | 90 s | Easy | [Leg extensions](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_95-vz83X4M) |
| 6 | **[M]** Hip abduction | Glute med/min | Machine | 3 × 12–20 | 60 s | Easy | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=hip+abduction+machine+form) |
| 7 | **[M]** Standing calf raise | Gastrocnemius | Machine | 3–4 × 10–15 | 60 s | Easy | [Calves](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-qsRtp_PbVM) |
| 8 | **[O]** Hip thrust | Glutes | Barbell | 3 × 8–12 | 2 min | Moderate | [Hip thrust tutorial](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=z8WwUC_UA4w) |
| 9 | **[O]** Adduction machine or Copenhagen plank | Adductors | Machine/BW | 2–3 sets | 60 s | Easy–moderate | [Copenhagen planks](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YRRnnZsRs9U) |
| 10 | **[O]** Seated calf raise | Soleus | Machine | 3 × 12–20 | 60 s | Easy | [Calf rehab progressions](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Q8egrbpRO2U) |

**Progression:** double progression; increase load on 1–3 when all sets hit the top of the rep range. **Recovery:** this is a high-volume session; don't train heavy legs again within 72 h.

## 20.3 C. Two-day lower-body split (intermediate; e.g., Monday and Thursday)

**Day 1 — Quad and glute focus**

| # | Exercise | Sets × reps | Rest | Notes | Video |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **[M]** Back squat or hack squat | 4 × 6–8 | 3 min | Main lift | [Squat form guide](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8Kls95w2jFA) |
| 2 | **[M]** Leg press | 3 × 10–15 | 2 min | Feet mid-low | [Leg press mistakes](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4eTMx8Epkks) |
| 3 | **[M]** Walking lunge | 3 × 10 / leg | 2 min | Long stride | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=walking+lunge+dumbbell+form) |
| 4 | **[M]** Leg extension | 3 × 10–15 | 90 s | Pause at top, slow down | [Leg extensions](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_95-vz83X4M) |
| 5 | **[M]** Hip abduction | 3 × 15–20 | 60 s | Lean forward slightly | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=hip+abduction+machine+form) |
| 6 | **[M]** Seated calf raise | 4 × 12–20 | 60 s | Stretch pause | [Calf rehab progressions](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Q8egrbpRO2U) |
| 7 | **[O]** Copenhagen plank | 2 × 20–30 s / side | 60 s | Short lever first | [Copenhagen planks](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YRRnnZsRs9U) |

**Day 2 — Hamstring and glute focus**

| # | Exercise | Sets × reps | Rest | Notes | Video |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **[M]** Romanian deadlift | 4 × 6–10 | 3 min | Main lift | [RDL tutorial](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5bJEigM5iVg) |
| 2 | **[M]** Hip thrust | 3 × 8–12 | 2 min | 1 s pause at top | [Hip thrust tutorial](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=z8WwUC_UA4w) |
| 3 | **[M]** Seated leg curl | 3 × 8–12 | 90 s | Lean forward | [Leg curl setup](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=S367qaHeYWU) |
| 4 | **[M]** Bulgarian split squat | 3 × 8–10 / leg | 90 s | Long stance, slight lean | [BSS tutorial](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hiLF_pF3EJM) |
| 5 | **[M]** Adduction machine | 3 × 12–15 | 60 s | Full stretch | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=hip+adduction+machine+form) |
| 6 | **[M]** Standing calf raise | 4 × 10–15 | 60 s | Stretch pause | [Calves](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-qsRtp_PbVM) |
| 7 | **[O]** Nordic curl (assisted) | 2–3 × 4–8 | 2 min | Build slowly | [Nordic curls](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_e9vFU9-tkc) |
| 8 | **[O]** Cable hip flexion | 2 × 10–12 / leg | 60 s | Hold at top | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=cable+hip+flexion+psoas) |

**Weekly volume** ≈ 12–18 hard sets for quads, hamstrings and glutes — within the range where most people continue to progress (Schoenfeld et al., 2017). **Recovery:** at least 2 days between the sessions.

## 20.4 D. Optional accessory session — calves, ankles and feet (≈20–25 min, 2× per week)

Can be done at the end of a leg day, on an upper-body day, or at home.

| # | Exercise | Target | Equipment | Sets × reps | Rest | Video |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **[M]** Single-leg calf raise on step | Gastrocnemius, soleus | Step, DB | 3 × 12–15 / leg | 45 s | [Calf rehab progressions](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Q8egrbpRO2U) |

| 3 | **[M]** Banded eversion | Fibularis | Band | 2 × 15–20 / side | 30 s | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=banded+ankle+eversion) |
| 4 | **[M]** Banded inversion | Tibialis posterior | Band | 2 × 15–20 / side | 30 s | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=banded+ankle+inversion) |
| 5 | **[M]** Single-leg balance (progressive) | Ankle stabilisers | BW | 3 × 30 s / leg | — | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=single+leg+balance+progression) |
| 6 | **[M]** Short-foot | Arch intrinsics | BW | 2 × 10 × 5 s | — | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=short+foot+exercise) |
| 7 | **[O]** Ball-squeeze heel raise | Tibialis posterior | Ball | 2 × 12 | 45 s | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=tibialis+posterior+heel+raise+ball) |
| 8 | **[O]** Toe yoga | Toe control | BW | 2 × 10 cycles | — | [Toe yoga](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bUoTjK0tQEw) |
| 9 | **[O]** Toe spreading + big-toe press | Intrinsics | BW | 2 × 10 each | — | [search](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=toe+spreading+big+toe+press) |
| 10 | **[O]** Knee-to-wall rocks | Ankle mobility | BW | 2 × 10 / side | — | [Ankle mobility](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7hrTiJIqvr4) |

**Progression:** longer holds → harder balance conditions → single-leg → added load. **Recovery:** low fatigue; can be done on consecutive days if needed.

# 21. Functional strength and running support

| Function | Key muscles | Why strength matters | Supporting exercises |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Walking** | Glute med, calves, tibialis anterior, hip flexors | Pelvic level in single stance; push-off; foot clearance | Hip abduction, calf raises, tibialis raise, hip flexion |
| **Running** | Soleus, gastrocnemius, quads, glutes, hamstrings | The calves absorb and return large forces each step; hamstrings decelerate the swinging leg | Seated calf raise, single-leg calf raise, Nordic curl, split squat, step-up |
| **Stair climbing** | Quads, glute max, calves | Single-leg knee and hip extension | Step-up, BSS, leg press |
| **Single-leg stability** | Glute med/min, adductors, foot and ankle | Controls knee alignment and pelvis | Single-leg RDL, step-up, Copenhagen, balance |
| **Balance** | Ankle stabilisers, foot intrinsics, hip abductors | Small corrective forces | Single-leg balance progression, short-foot |
| **Hip stability** | Glute med/min, deep rotators | Keeps the femur centred and the pelvis level | Abduction, rotation series |
| **Knee control** | Quads, hamstrings, glute med | Limits excessive inward collapse | Split squat, step-up, lateral walk |
| **Ankle mobility** | Calf flexibility, joint mobility | Needed for squat depth and running mechanics | Knee-to-wall, calf stretches |
| **Foot arch strength** | Abductor hallucis, FDB, tibialis posterior, fibularis longus | Dynamic arch support during stance | Short-foot, ball-squeeze heel raise |
| **Toe control** | FHL, FHB, lumbricals | Big-toe stiffness for push-off | Toe yoga, big-toe press |
| **Ground contact** | Calves, foot stiffness, quads | Short, stiff contacts improve running economy | Calf raises, heavy compounds; plyometrics (advanced) |

Strength training is associated with improved running economy (Balsalobre-Fernández et al., 2016; Blagrove et al., 2018) and with fewer sports injuries overall (Lauersen et al., 2014). For runners, the most transferable choices are single-leg work (split squat, step-up, single-leg RDL), heavy calf work (both straight and bent knee), hamstring eccentrics and foot/ankle stability. This document does not include a running programme.

# 22. Final recommendations

1. **Build around five patterns:** squat/press, hinge, leg curl, single-leg, calf raise.
2. **Add leg extensions** if you want complete quadriceps development — squats underdevelop the rectus femoris.
3. **Choose the seated leg curl** over the lying curl when both are available.
4. **Train calves with a stretch pause**, and include a bent-knee (seated) version for the soleus.
5. **Add one hip-abduction exercise every leg day.** It's the single biggest gap in most programs.
6. **Do the 20-minute foot-and-ankle session twice a week**; it's low effort and fills nearly every remaining gap in the coverage matrix.
7. **Progress conservatively** with double progression and deload every 6–10 weeks.
8. **Prioritise effort and consistency** over finding the "perfect" variation; similar exercises are close to ties.
9. **Use the videos as technique checks**, starting with Squat University for squats and hinges, E3 Rehab for Nordic, Copenhagen and leg extensions, and Jeff Nippard for evidence context.

| Toes | [Toe Yoga? A Physical Therapist Explains and Demonstrates](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bUoTjK0tQEw) | Congruency Therapy & Wellness | PT demo | Toe dissociation |
| Front squat ★ | [How To Front Squat With Perfect Form (Stop Making These Mistakes)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=G-Vamqoy8qM) | Squat University | Full tutorial | Rack position, elbows, mistakes |
| Hip thrust ★ | [How To Hip Thrust (Proper Form And Mistakes To Avoid)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=z8WwUC_UA4w) | Markow Training Systems | Full tutorial | Bench height, foot position, lockout |
| Leg curl machines ★ | [How to Use Leg Curl Machines (Prone & Seated)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=S367qaHeYWU) | Fitness 19 | Machine setup | Pivot and pad setup |
| Calves / soleus rehab ★ | [Calf Muscle Strain Injury (Best Exercises for Rehab and How to Return to Running)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Q8egrbpRO2U) | E3 Rehab | Rehab tutorial | Seated and single-leg calf progressions |

## 23.3 Video quality assessment

**How these ratings were made.** Instructor credibility is based on stated professional credentials. Completeness and beginner-friendliness are judged from each video's format and stated scope (full tutorial vs short demo vs evidence review). Clarity and technical accuracy can only be judged by watching, which this research could not do, so they are left for you to check with the viewer checklist below. **Popularity ≠ reliability:** large channels are listed because they are popular *and* credentialed, not because view counts prove accuracy.

| Video | Instructor credibility | Likely completeness | Beginner friendliness | Relevance to the exercise | Popularity vs scientific reliability |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Squat University — squat, front squat, RDL, single-leg RDL, warm-up, ankles, World's Greatest Stretch | **H** (Doctor of Physical Therapy) | H (full step-by-step tutorials) | H | H | Popular and clinically grounded |
| E3 Rehab — Nordic, Copenhagen, leg extension, calf strain | **H** (physical therapists) | H (setup, progressions, programming) | M–H | H | Smaller audience, high reliability, cites research |
| Jeff Nippard — quad ranking, hamstring tips, calves | **M–H** (science communicator, cites studies) | M (evidence and selection rather than step-by-step form) | M | M–H | Very popular; rankings mix evidence with opinion |
| Jeremy Ethier — science-based leg day | **M–H** (kinesiology degree, cites studies) | M (workout overview) | H | M | Very popular; accessible summaries |
| ATHLEAN-X — Bulgarian split squat | **M–H** (physical therapist, strength coach) | H (form-focused) | M | H | Very popular; strong opinions — cross-check with other sources |
| Dr. Dave Candy — leg press mistakes | **H** (physical therapist) | M–H (mistakes and fixes) | H | H | Smaller channel, clinically grounded |
| Alan Thrall — coaching the squat | **M** (experienced strength coach, Starting Strength model) | M (live coaching session) | M | M (low-bar style specifically) | Popular in strength sport; coaching opinion |
| Markow Training Systems — hip thrust | **M** (strength coach) | H (form + mistakes) | H | H | Coaching opinion; compare with Bret Contreras's own channel |
| Fitness 19 — leg curl machines | **L–M** (gym-chain trainer) | M (machine setup only) | H | H | Basic but useful for first-time machine setup |
| MEND Colorado — Spanish squat | **H** (physical therapy clinic) | M (single-exercise demo) | H | H | Small channel, clinically grounded |
| Dr. Joel Seedman — wall shin raises | **M** (PhD exercise physiology) | L–M (short demo) | H | H | Coaching opinion |
| Congruency Therapy & Wellness — toe yoga | **H** (physical therapist) | M (explanation + demo) | H | H | Small channel, clinically grounded |

**Viewer checklist (use on any tutorial).** Does the instructor (1) show the full setup, including foot and grip position? (2) explain *why* each cue matters? (3) show the most common mistakes and how to fix them? (4) give an easier option for beginners? (5) avoid claims that one variation "isolates" part of a muscle? (6) acknowledge that body proportions change what good form looks like? A video that passes all six is worth trusting.

## 23.4 Educator guide

| Educator / channel | Best for | Credibility note |
|---|---|---|
| [Squat University](https://www.youtube.com/@SquatUniversity) (Dr. Aaron Horschig, DPT) | Squat, hinge, mobility, pain | Physical therapist; technically precise |
| [E3 Rehab](https://www.youtube.com/results?search_query=E3+Rehab) | Nordic, Copenhagen, leg extension, rehab | Physical therapists; strong programming detail |
| [Jeff Nippard](https://www.youtube.com/playlist?list=PLp4G6oBUcv8w8ujRtP5BtvJe8PXBwiTdl) (tier-list playlist) | Evidence summaries, exercise rankings | Cites research; rankings include personal opinion |
| [Jeremy Ethier](https://www.youtube.com/@JeremyEthier) | Science-based workout explainers | Kinesiology background; accessible |
| [Renaissance Periodization](https://www.youtube.com/@RenaissancePeriodization/videos) | Hypertrophy programming, technique | Sport-science PhDs; entertaining style can exaggerate |
| [Bret Contreras](https://www.youtube.com/c/bretcontreras1) | Glute training, hip thrust | PhD; glute research specialist |
| [Alan Thrall](https://www.youtube.com/channel/UCRLOLGZl3-QTaJfLmAKgoAw) / Starting Strength | Barbell squat and deadlift coaching | Experienced coach; low-bar strength focus |
| ATHLEAN-X | Form tutorials | Physical therapist (Jeff Cavaliere); strong opinions — cross-check |
| Muscle and Motion | 3D anatomy animations | Excellent for visualising muscle action; no specific video verified here |
| Juggernaut Training Systems | Powerlifting technique | Strength-sport focus; no specific video verified here |
| Muscle & Strength | Exercise demonstrations | Broad library; quality varies; no specific video verified here |

**Popularity is not reliability.** A widely viewed video is not necessarily accurate. Prefer instructors who explain *why* a cue matters, show common faults, and acknowledge individual variation.

## 24.1 Review log (2 November 2026)

A second review against the master prompt found and fixed these gaps:

| Gap found | Fix |
|---|---|
| Sartorius, psoas minor, articularis genus and flexor digiti minimi brevis missing from the anatomy and coverage matrix | Added to Sections 2.3, 2.4, 2.8 and Chapter 19 |
| Dumbbell Squat (named in the prompt) had no full entry | Added as §4.12 |
| "Reverse calf raise" and toe flexion/extension not explicitly named | Named as aliases on §9.1, §11.2, §11.4, §11.5 and in §14.6 |
| Exercises not grouped into the prompt's 12 categories | Category index added (§2.11) |
| Rankings lacked the nine assessment criteria | Nine-criterion tables added (§15.6) |
| No video quality assessment | Added (§23.3), with an honest note on what could and couldn't be judged |
| Programs B–D and parts of the warm-up and cooldown lacked video references | Video column added to every program, warm-up and cooldown row |
| Equipment table showed "Machine required: Yes" for exercises where the machine is optional (e.g., Bulgarian split squat, Nordic curl, tibialis raise) | Now shows "Optional" |
| Several entries were thin (e.g., lying leg curl, donkey calf raise, front squat, GHR, foot drills had "—" cells or a single mistake) | Rewritten with full execution steps, tempos, parameters and at least two mistakes each |
| Maeo et al. (2021) author list was wrong | Corrected against PubMed Central |
| Leg-extension evidence claim lacked a specific source | Now cites Kubo et al. (2019), where squats did not grow the rectus femoris |
| Only general anatomy links | Region-specific illustrated anatomy links added to each anatomy table (all checked) |
| 20 verified videos | 24 verified videos (front squat, hip thrust, leg curl setup, E3 Rehab calf progressions added) |

**Still not possible:** verified Shorts links (YouTube Shorts pages expose no title or channel to automated checks) and embedded exercise or machine photographs (none could be confirmed as accurate and licensed for reuse). Image-search links are provided instead.
